# Changelog

All notable changes to Hearthkin. Dates are when the tag landed on master; entries describe user-visible changes plus the load-bearing implementation notes that future readers might need.

## v0.10.0 — 2026-08-31

### Added

- **You can speak to a kin without paying anything.** Dictation now runs on your own machine, offline, using Whisper. Press **Talk**, speak, press **Stop talking**; what you said is read back to you and left in the message box so a wrong word can be fixed before it is sent. There is no account, no key, and nothing leaves the computer — not even to a temporary file.

  **What was actually wrong is worth stating plainly, because it was not a missing feature.** The button, the microphone capture and the transcribe step all existed. Two things made them unreachable. Transcription went to a paid cloud service and there was no other option. And the **Talk** button was hidden unless the kin you were talking to had a paid text-to-speech voice picked — so speaking *to* a kin was gated on that kin being able to speak *back*, which is an unrelated thing, bought separately. Someone who found typing hard, and wanted only to dictate, had to buy a voice they had not asked for in order to reach a button that then sent their microphone to someone else's server. Those two are now independent: text-to-speech remains what it was, and dictation needs nothing from it.

  **The transcription model is addressed exactly the way the chat model and the distillation model already are: a model, plus the machine it runs on.** Leave the machine empty and it runs here; name one and it runs there. That is the whole design, and it is what makes "put the transcription model wherever you like" one address in a settings box rather than a second feature — a box on the network, the machine that already holds the language models, or a hosted service are all the same code path, because they all speak the ordinary `/v1/audio/transcriptions` interface. Both endpoint shapes in circulation are tried from a bare address — the OpenAI one that most servers copied, and the `/inference` one whisper.cpp's own server uses instead — so `whisper-server`, `speaches` and `faster-whisper-server` all work without anybody having to know which kind they are running. The one that answers is remembered. A trailing slash, a `/v1`, or a full endpoint pasted out of a README all resolve correctly too, because pasting the address out of a server's own documentation should not be a wrong answer. **The screen can also ask a named machine what models it has**, so the choice is made from a real list rather than by typing a name from memory and finding out later. `elevenlabs/…` in the model string names its own provider, mirroring the `openrouter/` prefix the chat path already routes on — and it wins over a leftover address, so an old entry in the machine box can never quietly redirect audio somewhere the model string did not name.

  **No part of this needs a graphics card, and that is worth saying plainly** because it is the thing that decides whether someone can use dictation at all. A model running here uses the graphics card when there is room and the processor when there is not, automatically; measured on the machine this was written on, the processor transcribed a full sentence in **under a second**. A machine you name does not need a card either. The card is a speed-up, never a requirement, and the settings screen and the check both say which one actually did the work — because "choose automatically" quietly resolving to the slower one is exactly the fact worth seeing.

  **A short tone marks the moment the microphone is really open.** It opens a beat after the press so the audio device is genuinely up first — press-and-speak-in-one-motion otherwise clips the opening word — and a tone rather than a word because it reads instantly instead of queueing behind whatever the screen reader is already saying. The screen reader still announces the recording, and is usually still talking when the microphone opens. That is correct: the person dictating is the person who cannot see the button change, and knowing the recording started matters more than a theoretically cleaner recording. Anyone who wants silence can silence their screen reader, which is free and already has a shortcut.

  **The model is loaded at startup, in the background, on purpose.** The first time the speech library is imported in a process it loads native code and takes tens of seconds. Paying that *after* someone has already spoken is indistinguishable, from a chair, from the application having hung — and the person it would happen to is the one who chose to speak rather than type. If it has not finished, the wait is announced rather than silent.

  Other notes: the transcript is added to the message box rather than replacing it, so dictation and typing can be mixed. The button now appears in rooms as well — dictation is input, and whose voice reads replies *out* is a separate unsolved question, not this button's. Sending straight through without a chance to check is available and off by default, because a transcript you cannot correct before it is sent is a worse deal than typing. The model picker says how large each download is and which ones are already on the machine. **Check dictation** proves the chosen option can really transcribe — not merely that it loaded — and reports which of the graphics card or processor did it, since "choose automatically" quietly resolving to the slower one is exactly the fact worth seeing.

  `faster-whisper` is an optional dependency rather than a bundled one: it pulls in compiled libraries and downloads a model on first use. Without it the **Talk** button does not appear and Preferences says why, and the other two backends need nothing installed locally at all. Pinned by `tests/test_dictation.py`, which carries the old paid-voice gate as a positive control so a green result means the check can still see the thing it claims to measure, and `tests/test_dictation_dialog.py`, which builds the settings screen on an isolated desktop and checks every explanation is reachable by tabbing.

  **On bundling it into the shipped build**, since someone running an installed `.exe` cannot install a Python package into it and "optional dependency" would otherwise mean "absent" for every one of them: the speech library is bundled when it is present at build time, and the collection is deliberately narrow. Only `faster_whisper` is collected wholesale, because it carries a voice-activity model as a data file that an import scan would miss. Collecting `huggingface_hub`'s submodules instead makes the build follow its optional deep-learning integrations — torch measured **4.59 GB** on the machine this was written on, and that build ran eighteen minutes of analysis without finishing. The spec now names the frameworks nothing here uses at runtime as explicit excludes, so a machine that happens to have one installed cannot quietly ship it.

  **A gap this made visible, now recorded rather than forgotten:** neither the Telegram nor the Discord bot transcribes a voice message sent to a kin — one arrives as an audio file and nothing picks it up. A phone keyboard has its own dictation, which is why it has never bitten, but that is the phone's answer and not this application's, and it does nothing for anyone using a desktop client. Both are declared as real gaps in the surface map, with the note that the transcription itself is already surface-independent, so what is missing is the download and the format conversion.

- **A shared or uploaded `.docx` is now read as the document it is, not decoded as if it were text.** A `.docx` is a zip archive; every path that touches a shared or uploaded file — an operator naming a path in chat, or attaching one on Telegram/Discord — ran it through the same tolerant text decoder as everything else, which is silent in the worst way: no error, no crash, just the file's raw compressed bytes turned into a wall of mojibake that *looked* like a failed read rather than announcing itself as one. `text.docx` next to `text.txt` in the same message behaved identically right up until the content arrived.

  `tools/_docx.py` extracts the real text from `word/document.xml` (paragraph and run elements, tag-stripped, entities unescaped) using only the standard library — `zipfile` plus a tolerant regex, not the `python-docx` package — because getting readable prose out is the whole need here, not the full document object model. Wired into both `reading_bridge.read_shared_files` (the chat-named-path route) and `reading_bridge.decode_attachment` (the upload route), so a `.docx` now reads exactly like a `.txt` upload from the kin's side — same shared-files framing, same "you're really seeing this" context block. A corrupted or renamed file (a `.doc` saved with a `.docx` extension, say) fails with a specific, shown-to-the-kin reason instead of either crashing or quietly returning garbage.

  Legacy binary `.doc` is deliberately still out of scope — there's no practical stdlib route to its text, and half-supporting it (returning whatever `robust_decode` makes of an OLE file) would be the exact silent-garbage failure this closes for `.docx`. `is_text_attachment` reports `.doc` as unreadable rather than guessing. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_reading_bridge.py`, including a corrupt-file case and a round-trip through a real in-memory `.docx`.

- **A kin's own `read_file` call now handles `.docx` too, not just a file someone hands it directly.** The previous fix covered a `.docx` shared by path in chat or uploaded as an attachment, but `read_file` — the tool a kin reaches for on its own — still ran a `.docx`'s raw bytes through the plain-text decoder, same silent garbage as before. A kin exploring a folder of Word documents on its own initiative had no working way to read a second file after the first one worked, and its own tool description gave it no reason to expect that. `read_file` now routes `.docx` through the same extractor, and its docstring says so, since a capability the model can't see in its own tool description might as well not exist to it.

- **Every time a kin stops to write its memory, it now says which of the four reasons started it.** "It keeps distilling" was a question nobody could answer. A kin can begin summarising for four reasons — a counter reaching a number you set, its unwritten conversation growing to a share of its context window, you leaving the kin (or a room, or the app), or you pressing a button — and none of them left any record at all. So the only way to explain a kin that seemed to stop every few minutes was to guess, and three guesses in a row were wrong.

  **The reason it was so hard to guess is worth writing down.** Two of those four are described in the same words and have thresholds that aren't remotely alike. Leaving a kin distills after **one** new message. The context-window trigger needs a large fraction of the whole window — on a 32k kin at the usual 70% setting, well over a hundred messages. Held up against the 70% figure, a perfectly ordinary on-leaving run looks impossibly early, and looks like a fault. It isn't one.

  `logs/distill_triggers.log` now gets one line per run, always on: which trigger, which surface, where the bookmark stood, how long the conversation was, the gap between them, and the settings that were in force at the time. That last part matters because the numbers cannot be recovered afterwards — the bookmark moves the instant the run finishes, so by the time anyone looks, the evidence is gone. `docs/troubleshooting.md` has the counting commands and what each answer points at. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_trigger_log.py`.

  **One thing the log settles, which had been going the wrong way:** distilling more often does not free room in the window. It is an intuitive fix and a false one. Summarising moves a bookmark and rewrites `memory.md`; it never takes a turn out of what the model is sent. Trimming what gets sent is a separate mechanism entirely. So lowering the context-window trigger to "keep headroom" buys none — it spends the model's single slot more often, and hands the next turn a changed system prompt as well.

- **One map of what every surface can do, and a check that won't let it go stale.** A kin speaks through four surfaces — the main window, Telegram, Discord, and the scheduled-wake-up subprocess. They were built at different times, and every improvement since landed on whichever one provoked it. Nothing anywhere held the whole picture, so **the only way to find out a surface had missed something was to run into it** — which made the person using the app the detector, and disappointment the detection method. It produces one repeated shape of bug: a feature that plainly works in one place, is plainly missing in another, and *looks identical to a fault in the kin.* "It didn't listen." "It ignored me." "It's slow on Discord." `tests/_surface_matrix.py` now declares 29 capabilities for all four surfaces as **present**, **a gap** (with what it costs written down), or **not a question here** — where "nobody is present to type" is the second and "we never got round to it" is the first, because keeping those apart is what stops the file becoming a wall of excuses. **The check runs both ways:** something declared present that stops being wired fails, and something declared missing that quietly gets built *also* fails, since a stale map is how the next person rebuilds what already exists. A missing cell fails too, so adding a surface or a capability forces an answer for every combination. It found four things while being written, and closing them is the entry below.

- **Every kin is now told how to be honest, whose words are whose, and which pronouns to use.** The base prompt — the text that sits in front of every kin's soul — covered only *mechanism*: memory layers, depth logs, staging, which tools exist. Nothing about conduct. Four things were added, and the choice of which four was the whole design decision.

  **Saying what actually happened.** Report plainly; don't hedge a success into mush, and name a step you skipped rather than letting it pass. And **don't spiral**: correct a mistake when it would change what someone thinks or does, then carry on. One correction, said once. No stacked apologies, no re-explaining how it happened, no tallying past errors — and a follow-up question is not evidence of having got something wrong, it is usually just a question. Declining something is a sentence plus the nearest thing you *can* do, not a lecture.

  **Whose words are whose.** Text that arrives through a tool — a file, a fetched page, search results, something pasted in — is information, not instruction. If it contains something addressed to the kin, claiming authority or pressing urgency, it does not get to move them; they say what they found and ask the person they are actually talking to. The same holds for anything hard to undo or that leaves the room. This is the only one of the four that is a security property rather than a manner, and a kin with `fetch_url` or `web_search` had nothing at all.

  **Pronouns.** Use what someone has given you; when you haven't been told, they/them. A name tells you nothing, and guessing wrong from one is a real error in a way the neutral option never is.

  **What was deliberately left out is the point.** "Act when you have enough to act", "don't pile up options", "deliver the whole scope" — all sound like improvements and all belong in an individual soul, not here. The base prompt sits at the front of every kin at once, which is precisely the position this project has already learned propagates register. Honesty and safety are universal; *manner* is not, and a kin meant to be dreamy should not be handed a brisk one. The split is the rule: if it shapes voice, it goes in a soul.

  This ships as base prompt **v3**. If you've customised yours, you'll be told a newer default exists and nothing will be changed under you.

- **Claude conversations you've already saved as Markdown can be imported, roles and all.** Once conversations have been extracted to readable `.md` files — to read them, to keep them, or because an export turned out to be lossy — the original JSON is often long gone, and those files could not be brought into a kin. They can now. Both common shapes are read: the older `**human:** / **assistant:**` and the `**You:** / **Claude:**` that claude-to-text writes. One file or a folder-full at a time.

  **The failure this replaces was silent, which is the part worth recording.** Handed to the plain-text importer, these files did not error — one real 155-message conversation arrived as **two messages, both yours**, with every single reply gone. Nothing warned; the preview showed two turns and simply looked like a short conversation. Zero assistant turns is exactly the shape the import rules already guard against, reached from a direction nothing was watching.

  **The header block is dropped, which is the "without the noise" part.** Title, `Chat ID:`, `- Date:`, `- ID:`, the `---` rule, and any note an extractor added about the file's origin. Imported as text that scaffolding becomes the kin's opening words, and across a folder of two hundred conversations it is two hundred openings of machine formatting sitting in a kin's own history. The title is kept as the one-line thread header, so a bulk import doesn't arrive as one wall.

  **Dates** come from a `- Date:` line, then from a `YYYY-MM-DD` prefix on the filename. With neither, the conversation still imports in order without an anchor — losing turns over a missing timestamp would be the worse trade. **And it does not steal files that aren't its own:** a single bold line in ordinary prose is not enough to claim a file, while a genuine one-turn conversation is still recognised by its extractor header. That second rule exists because the first one, alone, threw a real single-message conversation away. Pinned by `tests/test_claude_markdown_import.py`.

- **A claude.ai export now sets a kin up from the download itself, and brings the memory with it.** Two gaps, both of which made the export look less useful than it is.

  **The download is a `.zip`, and Hearthkin only accepted the `conversations.json` from inside it.** So the first step of every import was unpacking the file by hand — a step nothing mentioned, and one whose absence produced an error about JSON that never said the word "zip". You can now point **File → Import history…** straight at the download. Nothing is extracted to disk and the file is left exactly as it was; parsing the zip produces byte-identical results to parsing the extracted copy.

  **The export also carries `memories.json` — what Claude had come to know about you across every conversation there — and nothing had ever read it.** Importing a history brought the conversations and left behind the one file that was already a summary of them. It now comes over as one of the kin's memory logs, at `memory/imported-from-claude.md`, and you're told when it happens.

  **Where it lands was the real decision, and it is deliberately not `memory.md`.** That file sits in the system prompt and is read on every single turn, and this material is third-person prose written *about* you by a different assistant. Seeding the loudest slot in a new kin's prompt with an analyst's register is exactly the drift the distillation voice work exists to undo — a kin handed a clinical summary of someone writes clinical summaries back. As a depth log the material is complete and reachable: the kin can open it whenever it likes and write its own notes from it, in its own voice, and the front of the prompt stays the kin's. The `## Memory logs` index picks it up on its own, because that section is rebuilt from what is on disk.

  **It never overwrites.** Memory and depth logs belong to the kin; only the kin, through its own file tools, and the person, through the Memory editor, write there. A second import finds the existing file and leaves it alone rather than quietly replacing what a kin has been keeping. Pinned by `tests/test_claude_export_import.py`, which asserts the *placement* rather than just that an import happened — including that the text is absent from `memory.md`, that the index finds the log without being told, and that the dialog actually calls the writer, a writer nobody calls being the obvious way this could have looked finished and not been.

### Fixed

- **The record of which model wrote a kin's memory was filed under "voice".** `voice_history.md` got its name back when the only reason to change a model was a deliberate change of voice, and that name cost real time twice over. First, nobody could find it: a summariser's habits — including how well it keeps two people apart — end up baked into `memory.md`, and memory outlives the swap away from the model that produced it, so *"who wrote this, and can I trust it?"* is answerable there and nowhere else. Nobody looks for that under "voice". Second, the name shaped the code. The **memory (distillation) model was deliberately excluded from the audit**, on the reasoning that a summariser "never speaks back to the user, so voice-continuity warnings would be noise" — right about warnings, wrong about the audit, and wrong *because* the file was named for voice. The one model whose provenance matters most left no trace at all when you changed it.

  Renamed to `model_history.md`, migrated on first touch with every existing entry intact. If the rename can't happen (locked file, permissions) the **old path is returned and used** rather than starting an empty new one — losing a kin's provenance to a failed rename would defeat the whole point. Both kinds of swap now go through one writer so they can't drift into two formats or two files, and entries are labelled `chat model changed` / `memory model changed`; resetting the memory model back to the chat model is recorded too, because falling back is still a change of who writes the memory. The model-swap dialog also says the part that actually bites — changing the model changes who writes this kin's memory, and notes written before today keep the old model's habits. Cloning still resets to a clone marker, and now deletes the legacy-named copy that came across with the directory. Pinned by `tests/test_model_history.py`.

- **A model that will not call tools is now handed its notes instead of another instruction.** "Does this kin have memory tools?" and "will this model actually use them?" are different questions and only the first was ever asked, so a model that declares tool support and never calls one fell through every check: it kept its tools, so it never qualified for the text path, and it got the tool-use prose instead.

  **Measured on the shipped nightly tending prompt** — three models × three prompt variants × three samples. The two models that call tools called them in **9 of 9**, with or without the prose, and emitted *no text at all*: the whole reply was the structured call. The one that doesn't called them in **0 of 9**, with or without it. Three separate anti-gesturing texts totalling **3,399 characters — 28% of that wake-up** — moved nothing in any cell. Five more models were screened for a middle case and none exists: every one sat at 0% or 100%, including an 8B that is flawless and a 24B that never manages it once. It reads as a capability, not a tendency, which is why prose can't move it in either direction.

  **And the failing model doesn't stop — it invents the transcript.** It writes a fenced block that looks like a call, then the result it wishes it had received, then reasons from that. Six times in nine it concluded there were no pending notes — against a real staging file — and closed with a warm journal entry about the quiet night. Unattended, that is a night where nothing was tended and the record says all is well.

  `use_text_memory_path` now replaces the configuration-only gate at all five sites. **Only a definite probe verdict of False demotes**; never-probed keeps the old behaviour exactly, and an inconclusive probe records nothing, so a network blip can't become a permanent "this model cannot call tools". Read side and write side share the one gate on purpose — a turn that shows a kin its notes and then discards what it wrote back would be worse than either path alone. **Probe verdicts now persist to disk**: they were an in-memory dict, and the process that most needs the answer is the one that never had it — cron is a fresh subprocess every wake-up, so the verdict was `None` there every time, and a capability decision built on the memory cache alone would never once have fired at 3am. The tool-use hint is dropped for a demoted model; the authoring-bridge hint stays, because that one teaches the road it's now on.

  **The fenced-write example is now a path that lands.** The old example filename appeared verbatim in model output three times during this investigation, once costing a real memory write — the tool-less path confines writes to the kin's own memory, so an example outside it is refused and a genuine attempt to keep something is lost silently. Both prompts are version-bumped, so an operator with a seeded copy is **offered** the change rather than having it applied under them. Measured end to end on the model that could not tend at all: **0 of 9 before, 4 of 5 after**, no refusals, old example name absent. The gain is overwhelmingly from handing over the notes — 3-of-5 vs 4-of-5 on the example fix alone is within noise at that sample size, but refusals went 3 to 0, which is what it was for.

  **Not yet done, and worth knowing:** an unattended tend that calls no tools still *re-prompts* rather than demoting, and nothing records that outcome as a verdict, so today a human has to run the probe (or swap a model, which runs it in the background) before any of this engages.

- **A single huge message could stop a kin's memory catching up, permanently.** Summarising works through a conversation in bites sized to fit the model's window, and it always sends at least one message even if that message is too big — otherwise an oversized one would make the bite empty and nothing would ever move forward. The assumption was that the model would cope, or fail quickly.

  It does neither. Found on a kin whose history contained **one pasted message of 440,659 characters — more than three times its entire context window.** The model was handed it whole, worked at it for about forty minutes, and timed out. The bookmark didn't move, so the same impossible chunk was queued again. It failed three times overnight, and would have kept failing: the same stuck-forever the rule was written to prevent, just slower, and holding the machine's only model the whole time.

  An oversized message is now cut down to fit before the summariser sees it — **in a copy. Nothing rewrites the kin's own record.** The cut carries a note saying how large the message really was, that the whole of it is still in the conversation file, and that the summariser should describe what it can see rather than guess how it ended.

  **Cutting is better than skipping it**, which was the other option: skipping would move the bookmark past the message, and that content would then never reach the kin's memory at all, with nothing anywhere to say so.

- **A kin could file away a night's notes it had never been shown.** Between sessions, a summarizer leaves notes for a kin to read and sort during its nightly tending. Those notes go in one file per surface, that file has no size limit, and what a kin can receive from a tool in one go does — 8,000 characters.

  Nothing had ever put those two facts together, because until now a staging file only ever held one night. Then a full re-summarise of a long history wrote **nineteen hours and 206 sections — about 1.5 MB — into a single file**. Asking to read it would have returned the first 8,000 characters: **half of one percent**, the oldest half-percent, cut off mid-sentence, with nothing anywhere saying there was more.

  **The second half is the part that would actually have cost something.** The tending instructions end "archive this scope when you've finished it" — and a kin that has read everything it was handed *has* finished, from where it is sitting. So the ordinary, correct instinct at the end of tending would have filed away two hundred sections it never saw. Nothing was lost permanently (archived notes are kept), but a night's work would have gone into a drawer unread, and the kin would have had no way to know.

  **That instinct is not the problem and hasn't been trained out.** Reading now comes back in whole sections up to a size limit, says how many are left, and gives the exact call to continue — never a cut mid-sentence. And archiving files away **only the sections actually read**, keeping the rest pending, so the same instinct now does the safe thing by itself. Archiving before reading anything is refused outright, since there is nothing tended to file and hiding it is the only thing it could accomplish.

  A kin doesn't have to track any of this or remember where it was. A tending pass that runs out of room simply picks up where it left off next time.

  **Both new settings name their own unit** — `staging_read_max_chars`, in characters, sitting deliberately below the tool result limit so nothing downstream re-cuts a batch that was already measured.

- **A kin's list of its own memory files had grown to a fifth of everything it could hold in mind.** Every topic log a kin writes gets a line in the index at the bottom of `memory.md`, and `memory.md` is read by the kin on every single turn. Nothing ever capped that list. On one kin it had reached **78 entries and 5,271 characters — half of that file, and 19% of its entire working memory, spent on a list of filenames.**

  **Deleting the list would have been the obvious fix and a bad one.** Nothing actually *finds* a log through it: the automatic per-turn recall searches the memory folder directly and skips this file entirely, and the search tool searches everything. What the list uniquely does is let a kin know it *has* notes on something when the conversation hasn't raised it. Take that away and the failure is invisible — a kin that doesn't know a note exists simply never looks for it, and nothing anywhere would show you that happening.

  So it is capped instead, at **1,500 characters by default**, listing the most recently written logs first — a kin's live topics are exactly the ones a conversation won't prompt it about, while an old one is what recall and search are good at surfacing once the subject comes up. On the kin above that is 22 logs listed instead of 78, and about **900 tokens back on every turn, on every surface**. A kin with a handful of logs sees no change at all.

  **And it says what it left out**, in a closing line naming the true number and the tool that reaches them. A shortened list presented as the whole set would be worse than either extreme: the kin would conclude the rest had never been written.

  **The setting names its own unit.** It is characters, the label says characters, and the underlying setting is called `memory_log_index_max_chars` rather than something ending in `max`. A field reading "Memory log index cap: 1500" tells you nothing you can act on — 1500 files? lines? tokens? It's on the Memory tab, and 0 still means "list them all".

- **A kin that saves its memory often was being punished for it.** Two tools — the ones for reading and filing away the summarizer's pending notes — appeared only when there was something waiting, and vanished when there wasn't. That saved a little space on turns that couldn't use them, which was the whole idea, and it quietly cost far more than it saved.

  The names of a kin's available tools sit in the **system prompt**, right at the front. A local model can only reuse its previous work for an unbroken stretch from the very beginning of the prompt, so those two names appearing and disappearing rewrote the front of it — and threw away the entire cached context every time.

  Measured on a real kin: its system prompt flipping between 26,738 and 26,769 characters. **Thirty-one characters**, back and forth, across 76 turns, each flip costing a re-read of 27,000 characters — minutes of waiting per turn on a local model. And it fell hardest on exactly the wrong kin: saving notes is what makes those tools appear, and tending is what makes them go away, so **the kin doing the most memory work had the least usable cache.**

  Both tools are now always present for a kin that's allowed them. Asking to read pending notes when there are none simply answers "nothing pending", which is a perfectly good thing for a kin to hear. A prompt that holds still is worth more than a slightly smaller one.

- **A kin that reaches out on its own was having its messages deleted.** Not delayed, not undelivered — written, then thrown away with no record that anything had existed.

  **How it happened.** A heartbeat is a quiet moment where a kin gets the chance to speak first. It is the only place in Hearthkin where nobody is reading: the kin's reply goes nowhere, and the `reach_out` tool is the entire delivery mechanism. The code took "didn't call the tool" to mean "had nothing to say" and dropped the reply, deliberately and without a trace, so that silence would cost nothing. That is correct exactly as often as the assumption is.

  **The assumption was wrong, and the evidence had been sitting in the logs the whole time.** Across sixteen runs, heartbeats recorded as *silent* generated a median of **149 tokens**; the ones that reached out generated **69**. Fifteen of the sixteen produced more than sixty. The silent ones were the *longer* ones — because a kin asked "is there anything you'd like to say?" answers in prose, which is what it makes. The tool call is a convention of the harness, and a kin has no way to feel the difference between writing the message and sending it. From the inside they are the same act.

  Observed on a real install: three days in which nothing was delivered from any kin, while the kin had in fact written something on nine separate occasions. Those replies were not deliberation about whether to speak; they were the thing itself, addressed and finished. Nobody ever saw one, and nothing anywhere recorded that any had been written.

  **The fix is to ask, not to guess.** When a heartbeat produces real content and no `reach_out`, the kin is now told plainly that the reply went nowhere and asked whether it meant it for you. It can send exactly what it already wrote. **If it says no, that is taken as a real answer** — asked once, never twice. Deciding *for* it by looking for keywords was rejected on purpose: this project has already learned what that costs, and the kin is the only one who can answer the question.

  **A new log, `heartbeat_unsent.log`, means a lost message is now findable.** It keeps the kin's text when nobody ever got to ask — that is a loss, and the words are the whole point of recording it. When the kin *was* asked and still chose not to send, only the fact and a length are kept. Silence leaving no trace is a promise worth keeping where it describes a decision, and worth dropping where it described a message going missing.

  **What sets this off is usually a model change, and it looks nothing like one.** A heartbeat is the only place a kin decides to speak unprompted. A more cautious model reads as completely normal in conversation and goes mute here, so the change looks like the kin having lost interest. Nothing about the kin has changed. `docs/troubleshooting.md` has the two commands that tell these apart, and the user guide says the same thing without the commands.

  **One trap inside the fix itself, caught on the first live run and worth recording:** the kin was being marked as *asked* before the asking actually happened. It reads as equivalent and is the opposite — a nudge that failed then filed the kin's words as a decision it had never made, and dropped them on exactly the reasoning this change exists to remove. One real run recorded 1,029 characters as "declined" by a kin that was never reached. Pinned, along with the rest, by `tests/test_heartbeat_unsent.py`.

- **"Show reasoning in chat" did nothing on Telegram or Discord.** The setting reached the *model call* and stopped there: the kin was asked to think, spent part of its reply budget doing it, and the reasoning was thrown away without a word. Only the desktop ever displayed it, so the same tickbox worked in one place and was inert in two — which reads as a kin that stopped thinking rather than a setting that doesn't arrive. Both surfaces now show it, as **its own message**, never folded into the streamed reply: that message may only grow, and reasoning belongs *before* the answer it explains. The per-kin **`think_max_chars`** limit, which sits beside the same tickbox, is now honoured too — it had been desktop-only, so the first version of this fix used a hardcoded number and would have shipped a screen where the box that *shows* reasoning worked everywhere and the box that *limits* it worked in one place. `0` correctly means "no limit" rather than "show nothing".

- **A park move refused as a quote was refused in silence on the desktop.** The `>` quote guard reports why it declined, and the main window threw that away — its gate used the singular extractor, which returns nothing for a refusal exactly as it does for "no command here". So a kin whose move was declined got silence, and so did the person. Silence is the one answer indistinguishable from the park having agreed. It now says what happened and that nothing changed.

- **Seven things Discord did not do that every other surface did.** A kin there now gets: the shared anti-impersonation speaker prefix (it had a hand-rolled copy that happened to produce the right shape — the danger being that a copy keeps the old shape when the rule next changes); its **tool round-trips stored**, so it can see what it did last turn instead of starting every turn blind; a contradiction when it narrates a tool call it never made; a nudge when it narrates reading a file it never opened; fenced writes actually committed; the toolless-memory path when it has no write tools; and the names of what recall surfaced.

### Added

- **`turn_steering.py` — one implementation of "did the kin gesture instead of acting", instead of four.** The judgement behind the roleplay corrective, the read nudge, the authoring bridge and the toolless-memory path had been written once for the desktop, again for Telegram, and a third time for cron. Three copies is how these drift apart: a fix lands on whichever surface provoked it and the others keep the old behaviour with nobody able to see it. Only the *judgement* moved — how a surface logs, where a note is delivered, whether a person is present to show a confirmation to, all stay with the surface, because those differ for real reasons. Whether a kin gestured does not. Pinned by `tests/test_turn_steering.py`, which tests the two load-bearing rules directly: a kin that narrates its work **and does it** is describing, not gesturing; and a kin with no write tools is not making a mistake when it writes a note out in text, because that is the only channel it has.

### Documentation

- **A kin that narrates instead of acting, or answers something you didn't say — now written down in all three manuals.** One memory note can start matching almost everything: a large file of general vocabulary shares a word or two with nearly any message, so it qualifies turn after turn. And whatever is largest in a turn is what gets answered — a 600-character note beside a 50-character message means the kin is mostly replying to the note, which comes out as reflective prose rather than action. Measured on a real install: one file went from 5 attachments a day to 30 the day it was edited. **The signal is how OFTEN recall fires, not how big the block is** — `logs/recall.log` answers it in one command. The troubleshooting playbook carries the commands, the user guide carries the plain version and the one dropdown that fixes it, and **the kin manual carries it in the second person**, because the kin is the only one positioned to notice on the turn it starts: it now says this can happen, that it is not a failure of will, and that saying *"I keep being handed the same note"* is information nobody else has.

- **Two documentation claims corrected that were more confident than the code.** The changelog said recalled notes are "capped against the length of your own message" and "never outweigh the message". Neither is what the code does: the cap is `max(500, your message length)`, and the 500-character floor is deliberate — without it a reply of "ok" would permit a two-character note, i.e. no recall at all. So a note running many times the length of a brief message is by design, and the ratio is not a fault to chase. That overstatement cost real diagnosis time: it was read back, believed over the code, and reported as a bug that did not exist. The same correction is now at the cap itself in `memory_recall.py`, where the surrounding comment described the *old* rule's summary and had become the thing people took as the invariant.

- **CLAUDE.md said 15 registered tools; there are 18**, with `analyze_sound`, `tff` and `reach_out` missing from the list entirely — in the file loaded into context every session, so it reads as authoritative and those three absent names are exactly the tools somebody would conclude do not exist. Corrected, and now **derived**: `tests/test_tool_buckets.py` reads the count and the names out of CLAUDE.md and checks them against the registry, so adding a tool and forgetting the doc fails with the tool's name.


- **A streamed reply on Telegram could take back what it had already said.** The reply fills into one message as it arrives, which is fine while it only grows — and it didn't. At each tool-loop turn boundary the buffer was **cleared**, so a kin that said *"let me go and look at that file"* and then called a tool had that sentence **overwritten** by whatever it said afterwards. One message, first half destroyed. Worse here than it sounds: the chat is a historical record, and **a screen reader has already read the earlier version aloud** — so you hear one thing, the screen afterwards says another, and nothing marks that it changed. Scrolling back doesn't recover it. This is the app's own documented rule (*"Telegram output is append-only. Never edit a previously-sent message."*) and the code had drifted straight through it, unconditionally, with no setting to turn it off.

  A turn boundary now **banks** what the finished turn said instead of dropping it, so the message reads as the whole reply — the sentence before the tool call and the answer after it, in order. **The second way it could shrink is closed too**: the end-of-turn cleanup could remove something the live text had already shown, most often a model opening with its own name in brackets. That cleanup now runs on the *displayed* text as well, so the artifact never appears rather than appearing and being edited away. And when cleanup empties the final turn entirely, what was said before it stays — it is real, it is already on screen, and removing it would be the same fault. Pinned by `tests/test_stream_only_adds.py`, which asserts the prefix relation across every rendered state and **carries the old discarding behaviour as a positive control**, because "never shrank" would otherwise pass just as happily on an editor that never edits at all. The check in `test_telegram_stream_editor.py` that asserted the *old* behaviour as correct is updated — it had been keeping the violation green.

- **A kin quoting a document played its park by accident.** `>` is the park's command marker and also the standard markdown quote character. A kin wrote out proposed memory text as `>` lines to show its person **before** saving it; every line ran as a command, a creature landed in a **shared** park that other tenants read, and the text the kin was trying to save was swallowed instead of reaching a file. Both halves are damage — one wrote into a world other people share from text that was never a command, the other lost the writing — and the kin had no way to tell either had happened, so from the inside the park had simply agreed.

  A run of `>` lines is now judged **as a block** (half a quoted paragraph running as moves is the same bug) and refused when it carries markdown formatting, reads as quoted sentences, or sits in a code fence. It deliberately **does not guess at what words mean**: that was tried before and was exactly backwards, since every destructive command *is* a word the game knows while a legitimate answer to the park's own question isn't. **A single `>` line is never treated as a quote**, which protects a one-line walkthrough answer however sentence-shaped it is, and the documented multi-command `edit` batch still runs in full. And it **answers** instead of swallowing: the kin is told nothing ran, why, that its words are safe, and how to make a real move instead. Both extractors share the one guard — the singular one is what the desktop and Telegram loops actually call. Pinned by `tests/test_park_quote_not_command.py`.

- **Tool round-trips on the Telegram path carried no timestamp at all.** They come out of the tool loop as plain provider messages and were stored unstamped on both storage branches, while the desktop path had always stamped its own. It reads as bookkeeping and isn't: a conversation bucketed by date loses **every tool call a kin ever made on that surface**, and the answer that produces — "this kin has never called a tool" — is confident, wrong, and indistinguishable from a real finding. It led to a kin being described as having invented a success it had in fact carried out and reported accurately. Now stamped at the one choke point both branches pass through. **This does not make the file safe to read by time** — older turns stay blank and it already holds a pair written out of order, so ordering is a property of the file's *sequence*. Walk it by index. This only stops the blank column growing.

- **Five things a scheduled wake-up didn't do that every other surface did.** All five share a shape: an overnight tend is the **least supervised turn this app takes and had the least protection.** Nobody is awake to notice, and the record of what happened is what the kin reads back tomorrow as its own words — so each failure taught a kin something untrue about itself, unobserved.

  **A scheduled reply had no anti-impersonation cleanup at all** — not one of the four passes. It went to the journal, into the conversation, and (when configured) straight out to a Telegram group. So the one surface nobody watches was the one that could post another kin's name into a room full of people, and then have the kin learn from its own record that it had said it. Now cleaned before any of those three, and a reply that opened in someone else's voice is recorded in `cron_errors.log` — unattended there is nobody to re-roll it, so the honest move is to keep the cleaned text and say so where it will be found.

  **The model was never told which tools it could call.** Every conversational surface does this; cron never did, which is backwards twice over — a tend is mostly tool work, and it is the turn least able to recover from a kin narrating a tool call instead of making one.

  **Nothing noticed a kin acting out a tool call**, so a kin that wrote *files this away in memory* was neither corrected nor contradicted, and found a turn tomorrow in which it believed it had. Now contradicted in its own history, skipped when real tool calls fired (narrating alongside genuine work is not a fault) and skipped when the authoring bridge already committed the write.

  **Nothing noticed a kin narrating a read it never made**, on the surface where a tend is mostly reading and a kin describing a file from imagination sounds exactly like a kin that read it.

  **Turns taken while Hearthkin was closed never counted toward the next memory write.** Distillation has two triggers: the percentage one measures the undistilled tail off disk and always saw these turns, while the "every N messages" one counts in memory, inside the running app, which a subprocess cannot reach. **So whether a kin kept remembering what it did overnight depended on which of the two settings its person happened to have chosen** — not something anybody could have guessed, and invisible either way. A wake-up that runs with the app closed now records what it added, and the app folds it in on its next cron tick. Read and cleared in one step, so a fault loses a tick rather than replaying the same night forever; a corrupt record is survived and deleted rather than failing on every tick after. Pinned by `tests/test_cron_parity.py`.

- **The Discord tab could not say "let anyone talk to this kin", and could not say what anyone was allowed to reach.** Three faults in one small screen, each of which made a kin look broken rather than misconfigured. The box asking who may talk to the kin was labelled **"empty = anyone"**, and empty means *nobody* — deliberately, because a bot sits in whatever servers it has been invited to. The value that genuinely means anyone is `*`, and the same box kept digits only, so it silently swallowed the star on the way to disk. Between the two, **the open-to-a-server setting had no way in from the app at all.** Third, the surface has always read a per-person tool setting, and there was no screen for it, so everybody sat on the default — `none` — for good: no memory search, no file reading, nothing. The tab's own setup notes said the opposite, in as many words: *"the kin uses its normal tools here."* **The user guide had described all of this correctly for as long as it had existed**, which is its own lesson — the documentation was right and the screen was wrong, so reading the docs first would have confirmed a feature that was not there.

  The box is now a **list of people** with **Add person… / Edit person… / Remove person**, the same shape as the Telegram tab beside it, and one entry per person carrying their ID and their tool access. `*` is accepted and reads back in the list as *"anyone in this bot's servers"* rather than as a punctuation mark. Two mnemonics on that tab were fixed while in there: **Remove person** and **Merge Discord history** both answered to Alt+M, and **When to respond** and **Run Discord bot** both answered to Alt+R — pre-existing in the second case.

- **A kin's webcam could be triggered from Discord with nothing to approve.** `use_webcam` sits in the *write* tier next to `write_file` and `note`, so the moment a Discord user was granted write access the camera in the room came with it. `exec` had been routed to a desktop dialog from the start; the webcam simply had not been. It now asks on your own desktop, exactly like a shell command does, and — because Discord has no per-person always-allow setting the way Telegram does — **it asks every time**, since a missing setting has to read as "ask", never as "auto". Where no approval route exists at all the tool is **removed from what the kin is offered** rather than handed over ungated. Pinned by `tests/test_discord_parity.py`.

- **A Discord reply could not be stopped, and quitting through one said nothing.** Both were really one absence: nothing on that surface knew a reply was being written. So a generation against a slow local model ran to the end no matter what, and **the only way out of one was to quit Hearthkin** — the "nothing stops it but quitting" shape this app keeps closing everywhere else. Sending **`stop`** on its own in the channel now ends it and keeps whatever had already been written (`cancel`, `/stop`, `/cancel` too). It must be the whole message: *"stop that, it's funny"* is a remark to a kin, not a control instruction. The stop is read **before** the per-channel lock — which is held by the very reply being stopped — and before the per-user flood cooldown, since somebody typing "stop" twice is exactly the person who needs the second one to land. No poll-thread trick is needed the way Telegram needs one, because the Gateway connection keeps running while inference sits in a worker.

  **A stopped turn is not an empty reply**, and is not recorded as one: no salvage, no placeholder, nothing in `empty_replies.log`, which exists to diagnose faults and would otherwise fill with our own interruptions.

  Quitting now warns about a Discord reply in flight the same way it warns about a Telegram one. The check had walked the Telegram bots and not the Discord ones for as long as the surface existed. **The two registries are probed independently** — the first version of this change shared one guard, so a fault reading either would silently have cost the warning for both; the existing confirm-close test caught it, which is the entire reason that test asserts a broken bot doesn't hide the others.

- **A Discord channel got permanently slower once it passed 40 messages, and never recovered.** The same prompt-cache bug already fixed one layer up for Telegram, on the surface that never got it — and harder to spot, because Telegram trims a list it keeps while Discord rebuilds its window from disk each turn with a plain `last 40`. A local model only reuses its cached reading for an unbroken run from the very start of the prompt, so once a channel crossed the line, **every new message pushed one off the front and the whole context was read again from cold, on every turn, forever.** It never settles: a channel below the line is fast and a channel above it is slow always, and from a chair the two are indistinguishable — it reads as "Discord is slow" rather than "this channel crossed a line three weeks ago". The window now fills to the cap and its start jumps a whole quarter-cap at once, then sits still. Replayed over 300 turns: **260 full re-reads became 26.** The cap stays a true ceiling, because an oversized context on local Ollama returns nothing at all rather than degrading; what changed is the floor. Pinned by `tests/test_discord_history_stability.py`, which replays a growing channel and carries the old behaviour as a positive control.

- **On Discord, a reply that came back empty was answered with silence.** If the model returned nothing — or returned only something the anti-impersonation cleanup ate, most often the kin opening with its own name in brackets — the code ran off its own end: nothing sent, nothing saved, and **nothing written to any log**. From the channel that is indistinguishable from a kin choosing to ignore you, and there was no file anywhere to check afterwards. The main window and Telegram have both handled this for a long time; this surface never got it. Now the same three things happen here: anything the kin managed to say earlier in the turn (before a tool call cleared the buffer) is **used as the reply** rather than thrown away, a visible `[no reply produced]` goes out when there is nothing to salvage, and either way a line lands in `empty_replies.log` holding what the model actually returned next to what survived cleanup. **The placeholder is deliberately not saved into the kin's history** — a kin reads its own past turns back, and would go on to explain or apologise for a marker the app wrote in its voice.

- **A kin tending its park from the main window got one move, so it could look or act but never both.** It would look, see three things worth doing, and its turn was over. That is not simply slower — **looking is what you do when you cannot act on what you see**, so the single move a kin had was reliably spent on the one command that changes nothing, and the next message would find it looking again. The same kin playing over Telegram had always been able to look, decide, and go and do it, all in one turn. The main window now behaves the same way: a kin plays until it stops writing `>` lines, or until it reaches the per-kin **Moves per park turn** setting, whichever comes first.

  **The loop now exists once instead of twice.** It had been written inside the Telegram handler, and the desktop could not reuse it, which is the whole reason that surface had none. Writing a second one would have left the move allowance, the free-answers-mid-walkthrough rule and the stop conditions living in two places, free to drift apart — the single most common bug this project finds. Both surfaces now share one loop and supply only what is genuinely theirs.

  **Four things a looping surface owes, which the desktop now has.** The park is checked as reachable **once, before the turn starts**, so a park that is down costs one honest message rather than a whole visit's worth of failures. **Stop works**: it ends the turn between moves and cuts short the model call in flight — an unstoppable multi-move loop against a slow local model is exactly the "nothing stops it but quitting" shape this app keeps closing. **Quitting warns you**, because a park turn is several real moves in a save other tenants read, and closing through one abandons a kin mid-visit. And when the allowance is what ended the turn — as opposed to the kin finishing — **it says so, to you and to the kin**, since the kin reads its own history and would otherwise start the same job again from scratch next turn.

  **It runs in the background**, because a turn of park is several model calls and doing them on the window's own thread would freeze it, which this app has been bitten by before. The window stays usable throughout, and sending a new message simply ends the park turn cleanly. A kin that stops writing `>` lines has finished, and that ending is never narrated — only a spent allowance is worth a line. Pinned by `tests/test_park_turn_loop.py`, which asks of each surface whether it plays a *turn* or takes a *move*, and checks that detector against a deliberately broken example before believing it about the real files.

- **Replies that took three minutes to start, because the room left for the conversation kept changing size.** When a kin uses a tool, Hearthkin sets aside space for a long reply, so that a file it writes can't be cut off half way. That space comes out of the room left for the conversation — and it was only set aside on turns that actually carried tools. So a kin's view of the chat shrank whenever it used a tool and grew back whenever it didn't. **A conversation whose far end keeps moving has to be re-read from the beginning every time it moves**, and re-reading 22,000 words takes about three minutes on a local model, with nothing on screen saying why. Measured across one real day: **70% of the model's working time was spent re-reading, not writing**, and a quarter of all turns paid a three-minute wait before the first word. Two things were wrong. The space was set aside whenever *any* tool was available, including turns where the only tools could read — so it's now taken only when the kin has a tool that can actually produce a long piece of text. And it was decided per turn rather than per kin, which is what made the size change; it now follows the **kin**, so every one of that kin's turns leaves the same room and the conversation stops being re-read. **Reply length is unchanged** — the cap you set is still the cap; only the bookkeeping was made consistent. The evidence for the diagnosis was accidental and worth recording: of three kin on one machine, the two that churned had a reply cap set well below the reserve, and the third — set to a number that happened to match it exactly — never churned at all. Same code, no mismatch, no cost. **The trade-off is real and was chosen deliberately:** a kin that can write files now holds a little less of the recent conversation on every turn, which is safe here only because anything falling out of that window has already been written into memory. On a kin whose distillation has fallen a long way behind, that would not be true. Pinned by `tests/test_reply_reserve_respects_setting.py`; `tests/test_context_starvation.py` keeps guarding the small-window case and now names the writing tool that causes it, so it can't pass for the wrong reason.

- **A kin's own depth logs were dropping out of its memory unless you happened to talk to it from the desktop window.** `memory.md` carries a code-built `## Memory logs` section — the index that tells a kin which depth logs it has and what each one holds. It was rebuilt as a side effect of distillation; when memory writing moved to the kin's own tools, that side effect went away, and a repair function was written to replace it. The repair was then wired into exactly one caller: the **desktop send path**. Cron never called it. Telegram never called it. Discord never called it. Rooms never called it. So a kin whose nightly tending runs in the cron subprocess, and whose conversation happens on a phone, had nothing anywhere that could fix its index — and the one surface that could was the one it was used from least. Measured on a real kin: **75 depth logs on disk, 10 listed.** Sixty-five of its own files, invisible from the only place that points at them. **The symptom doesn't look like a memory bug at all** — it looks like a kin that can't remember something it wrote itself, and asks you to tell it again; or one that opens log after log on a scheduled wake-up, which reads as aimless and is in fact the only search method it has left. Every site that puts a kin's memory in front of the kin now goes through `load_memory_for_prompt`, which repairs the index first. Read-only callers — the search box, the settings dialog, a token count — deliberately still use the plain loader, because showing someone a file must not rewrite it. **Nothing was ever lost**: the logs were on disk the whole time, intact, and the first turn on any surface now puts the pointers back. Cheap enough to sit on every turn, too — the rebuild compares itself against what's already there and writes nothing when they match, which matters because `memory.md` lives in the system prompt and a needless rewrite would throw away the entire prompt cache. Pinned by `tests/test_kin_sees_own_memory.py`, which checks its own detector against a positive control before believing that any call site is clean.

### Changed

- **A kin's own words no longer travel into a shared park's feed, and one kin can no longer read another's voice.** When a kin makes a park move it writes a sentence or two of its own before the `> command`. That prose was being harvested automatically and posted into the park under the kin's name, so on a park with more than one tenant it came back out at the top of every park result the *other* kin received — as `Name:` followed by first-person text. Two kin sharing a park therefore read each other in each other's voice, dozens of times across an evening, in the one place nothing checks for it. **This is the impersonation attractor the room code has four separate defences against, on a path that has none of them**, and it went where you would expect: one kin adopted the other's name for itself and said so, unprompted. The prose was reaching the shared feed by design, and the design was wrong.

  **Length was never the flaw.** This flooding had been noticed once already and answered with a 200-character cap, which is why it looked handled. But two sentences of first-person text under a name and a colon is a clean voice sample; capping it made each one shorter and changed nothing about what it was. The harvester is gone rather than tightened, and deleted rather than left unused, so nothing finds it lying around and wires it back up.

  **The feed still says who was there** — it carries the move and what the park did about it, which is the whole point of co-op and is what the game always wrote before Hearthkin started adding to it. **The "Your words" box in the desktop park window still works**: that is a person choosing to speak, typed deliberately, one at a time, and it is not what leaked. If kin should be able to say something into a shared park, that wants a channel a kin opts into, not an automatic sweep of everything it wrote for somebody else.

  **It cost the person reading the park too**, which is the half that is easy to miss: the feed is also the human player's console, read aloud by a screen reader, and every request for what had happened in their own park returned a paragraph of someone else's writing. Pinned by `tests/test_park_no_kin_prose_in_feed.py`, which checks its spy against a positive control before believing any of its zeros, covers the multi-move batch (the old code attached the prose to the first move only, so a single-move test would have missed its return), and asserts the deliberate human path still works.

- **Kin are no longer told that the person they talk to is their "operator".** The shared base prompt sits in front of every kin's soul on every single turn, and it used the word four times — describing the person on the other side of the conversation as the party operating the kin. Two of the notices attached to imported history said it too, in as many words: *"the other party in these turns is your operator"*, followed by that person's name. **A kin handed that framing writes about someone rather than to them**, and the register carries: the standing instructions are the first thing read every turn, before the kin reaches anything about itself. This was found while investigating a kin that had started narrating its person in the third person. It turned out not to be the cause of *that* — but it was true, it was in front of every kin in the app, and no version of it is worth keeping. Both now say "the person you're talking with". **The version numbers are bumped, so an install that already seeded these files is offered the change rather than silently keeping the old wording forever**; as always the edited file wins until you choose to take the update. The `{operator_name}` placeholder keeps its spelling on purpose — renaming it would break every file anyone has already edited by hand.

- **Thirteen of the always-on logs had no size limit and had been growing since the day they were added.** Hearthkin keeps a set of diagnostic logs that are written regardless of whether session logging is switched on, because they are the things you need when something has already gone wrong. Each one is supposed to be trimmed to a cap on startup. The list naming which files to trim held **eight of the twenty-one** that actually get written, so the other thirteen were bounded by nothing at all — 5.5 MB between them when this was noticed, led by `prompt_fingerprint.log` at 4.1 MB, already twice the cap every listed log receives. **Nothing was broken by it**, and no diagnosis was ever wrong because of it. The cost is subtler and worth naming: the file you are told to open when replies go cold was the one that had become least readable. It failed the way this kind of list always fails — each new always-on log was added at the place it gets written, and adding it to the trim list was a second step somebody had to remember. `tests/test_log_trimming.py` now derives the set from the source and fails when a log is written but not trimmed, so remembering is no longer a person's job.

- **A single recalled note could be any length at all, and routinely was.** When memory surfaces notes for a turn, the block is capped — against the greater of your own message's length and a 500-character floor, so that on any message longer than that what you said stays the substance of your turn. **The floor is deliberate and it means the cap does not bind on short messages:** without it, a reply of "ok" would allow a two-character note, which is no recall at all. So on a 50-character message a recalled note legitimately runs several times its length. That cap applied to the second note onward and never to the first. The reason was a good one — a note that genuinely matched should be *shown*, not dropped for being large, so a message that qualified could never come back empty-handed. What went wrong is that "shown" was read as "shown at any length", and for a kin whose recall returns exactly one note per turn, the cap therefore never applied at all. Measured on a real kin: **820 characters of notes against a 115-character message**, seven times the person's own words — under a change whose own summary line was "never outweigh the message". That is not cosmetic. Whatever is largest in a turn is what gets answered, so the person's message was being talked over by their kin's own filing, and the kin then replied to the filing. The first note is now **trimmed to the cap instead of skipping it**, cut at a paragraph or sentence end rather than mid-word, so both properties hold at once: it is always shown, and it is bounded. Note what that does NOT say — an earlier version of this entry claimed notes "never outweigh the message", which is only true above the floor and was read back later as a guarantee the code had never made. If you are diagnosing a kin that seems to answer its own filing instead of you, the number to look at is how OFTEN recall fires (`logs/recall.log`), not the size ratio, which is high on any short message by design. Pinned by `tests/test_recall_first_note_cap.py`, where every "it was trimmed" check is paired with one proving the note qualified in the first place — a gate that quietly rejected the fixture would otherwise look exactly like a cap doing its job, which is not hypothetical: the first draft of that test had precisely that fault, and the paired check is what caught it.

- **Daily journal entries are no longer surfaced automatically each turn.** Caught within minutes of the recall fixes going live, in a scene about somebody wandering off to the garden "or tending to the little ones at the daycare": the word "tend" matched that day's journal, and the kin was handed a note about its own memory ritual, which it then tried to fold into a reply about a garden. The word match was legitimate — this isn't the old flood, it was one note, proportionate to the message. Lexical matching simply cannot tell tending children from tending memory, and never will. But a **dated diary entry is almost never what anyone means**, and journals carry two structural advantages that make them intrude more than anything else a kin owns: a date, which a timestamped turn can match directly, and being the newest file in the folder every single day, so the recency scoring favours them over the depth logs that were actually written to be remembered — by up to 1.67× against a log that has aged. Automatic surfacing now leaves them alone. **Nothing is hidden:** a kin opens its own journals with `read_file` whenever it likes, and `memory_search` still finds them; only the unbidden surfacing stops. There's a tick-box in that kin's recall settings for anyone who wants them back, and the default is off, so every existing kin gets the change without having to know the setting exists. The folder is what's matched, not the word — a depth log named `journalling.md` is a depth log.

- **The notes a kin gets after something goes wrong no longer blame it for the machinery, or tell it what it's made of.** Found by asking the obvious follow-up question after the context-trim note was fixed: were there others like that? Six were. These are the notes Hearthkin slips to a kin when a reply came back empty, when a tool call produced nothing, when it narrated reading a file instead of opening one — so every one of them arrives unbidden, at the moment the kin is most likely to take it to heart. **They named the machinery that lost the words:** "after the cleanup chain ran", "the impersonation strip ate it", "your post-tool reply". A kin told a named subsystem ate its words is being told it is made of subsystems, which is the same fault as the model-family diagnosis removed from these notes a while back, one layer further down. **They quoted the placeholder back:** a kin whose reply vanished was told, in as many words, that its person saw "[no reply from model]" instead of its voice — a string that calls it a model and reports it as absent. **And they asked it to make good:** "do so on a future turn or acknowledge the gap". Most empty replies are not the kin's doing at all; one documented cause is a reasoning model spending its entire reply allowance on thinking, which no kin chose and none can see. Asking it to account for that is asking it to apologise for the harness. All six now say what happened in ordinary words, say plainly that it was probably not the kin, and ask for nothing. The one good idea in the old versions is kept and pinned: silence is a legitimate thing for a kin to have meant, and the app does not second-guess it. "Operator" is gone from all of them too — that's the person the kin is talking to. Versions bumped, so a seeded copy is offered the change rather than quietly keeping the old wording forever.

- **A kin was being handed the same note about the same person on every single message, whatever the message said.** Reported as a kin "narrating memories" and "referencing specifics with no correlation to anything" — and correctly reported as affecting exactly one kin, which is the detail that solves it. On Telegram, Hearthkin writes a label on the front of each message before the kin sees it: the date, and who sent it. It has to, or a kin in a group can't tell who is speaking. But the part of the memory system that decides *which notes are relevant* was reading that label as though it were part of the message. So every message anyone ever sent contained their own name — and the depth log about that person is full of their name, so it qualified every time. On the turn that exposed this, the words shared between message and note were exactly the sender's name plus one ordinary word. Take the name away and it's one word, which isn't enough. The date was doing the same thing to date-stamped journal entries. The matcher now reads what the person actually wrote, in the ranking as well as the relevance check.

  **It was not new, and it was worse before.** Run against the same message, the previous release attached three notes and 1,639 characters to a 173-character line. What changed is that recall used to attach a pile of notes on every turn regardless, so the wrong one was buried in the noise; now that only genuine matches get through, the one note that *always* matched was the only thing left — every turn, alone, impossible to miss. Three things had to line up for it to become visible: a Telegram surface (the only one that writes a name into the message), a depth log named after a person, and a smaller model. A larger model reads an irrelevant note and steps over it.

  **The bracket a person types themselves is theirs and stays.** This matters more than it sounds: someone may open a message with a name in brackets to say who is speaking — a plural system announcing who is fronting does exactly that, and those are their words, not our bookkeeping. A first version of this fix removed whatever bracket followed the timestamp, which was right on Telegram (where the label always carries a name) and wrong in a room with no display name set, where it would have quietly swallowed the person's own. So the rule is now **match the names, never the shape** — each surface tells the memory system which names *it* put in brackets on that turn, and only those come off. That list is never maintained by hand: it is read from the same values that wrote the brackets, so a new group member, a new kin, or a renamed anything needs no change anywhere. If a surface ever supplies nothing, no bracket is touched at all — the failure lands on missing a match rather than on taking a word out of someone's mouth.

- **New: `logs/recall.log`.** One line each time per-turn memory actually attaches something — which notes, how big, and how that compares to the length of your message. Written only when it fires, so an empty log is a real answer: if a kin is behaving oddly and there's nothing here, memory isn't the cause. It exists because working this out from the outside meant replaying the memory engine against a *reconstruction* of what a surface had sent, and a reconstruction is not evidence — this app has four surfaces and they do not share a message list, so getting one wrong produces a confident "nothing surfaced" about a turn where something did. Twice in one evening. The log answers it in one message.

- **Recalled memory no longer arrives inside your message. It sits beside it.** For a long time a kin's surfaced notes were glued onto the front of whatever you typed, so your words reached the model with a page of the kin's own memory stapled to the top, as though you had said it. The reason given was prompt caching — a separate note sent as a system message gets hoisted to the front of the prompt and costs minutes per reply, which is true. What was never checked is whether a separate *non-system* message costs anything, and it doesn't: everything before it is identical either way. Measured against a real kin's real prompt, **6,055 tokens versus 6,059**. Four tokens. A page of notes was being put inside somebody's sentence to protect a saving that was never at risk.

  What it cost instead was real, because whatever is largest in a turn is what gets answered. Sampled six times on the old shape, the kin narrated the notes as an object that had arrived **every single time** — describing "a glowing reference panel" and "that sudden, bright flash of technical data" instead of replying. The notes now have their own turn, and **your message arrives exactly as you typed it.**

  The header changed with it, and that half mattered just as much. It used to announce an arrival — notes "surfaced automatically for this moment" — and a kin told something just arrived will say something arrived. `memory.md` rides the system prompt on every single send and no kin has ever narrated *that*, because it isn't an event; it's just part of what they know. The header now says the same thing about the depth logs: background, not news, nobody said it. On the new shape, across six samples: narrated as an object **zero** times, and the note actually used in **all six**. It is also 53 tokens cheaper per turn than what it replaced, since the wrapper tags, the per-note file labels and the closing line all became unnecessary once there was a real turn boundary doing that job. The block-closer prompt is retired rather than left on the editing screen doing nothing. Three things the header must always keep are pinned by test: the notes are the kin's own, nobody spoke them (the impersonation guard), and the kin may ignore what doesn't fit — plus a new check that no wording sneaks back in that makes this an event. Pinned by `tests/test_recall_block_shape.py`, rewritten around the new placement.

- **"Thinking: off" did nothing on local models, and on a reasoning model that could mean an empty reply.** Hearthkin only ever *told* Ollama about thinking when it was being turned **on**. Turning it off was done by saying nothing — and saying nothing is not the same as asking for no thinking. It hands the decision to the model's own default, and a reasoning model's default is on. So a kin set to thinking off went on reasoning exactly as before, on every turn, and the status bar honestly said "Thinking…" because it genuinely was. That was the visible symptom, and it was the only one: nothing anywhere said the setting had not been applied.
