Caught live on a kin whose settings had thinking off: the model came back having generated its **entire** reply allowance — Ollama reporting `done_reason: "length"` with the full token count — and **nothing at all** in the reply, because all of it went into a reasoning block nobody sees. From a chair that is indistinguishable from a kin that had nothing to say, and it is one of the causes behind entries in `logs/empty_replies.log`. The setting is now sent explicitly either way, on all three paths that talk to Ollama. Checked against a non-reasoning model too, since the alternative — guessing which models can reason — is how this kind of thing comes back quietly. Nothing changes for a kin that had thinking on, or for anything routed through OpenRouter, which was always told properly. Pinned by `tests/test_ollama_think_off.py`, which carries the on case as a positive control so a spy that sees nothing can't pass as a fix.

- **Recalled memory was arriving on every message, whether or not the message had anything to do with it — often several times more text than the message itself.** This is the rest of the problem the previous entry treated as a formatting one. Reshaping the block was real and it helped, but the block should not have been there at all on most of those turns. Before each reply Hearthkin scores a kin's own depth logs against what's being discussed and attaches the best few to your message. Every step of that scoring was **relative** — each note scored as a fraction of the best note *that turn*, and the cutoff was a fraction of the same number. So when nothing matched, the best of a bad field set the standard and the standard dropped to meet it. There was no way for the engine to answer "nothing here is relevant", and so it never did. Replayed over 40 real messages for each of four kin, it surfaced memory on **160 of 160**. That is a quota, not recall.

  The second half is size. **How present** is a share of the kin's context window; at the default, on a 32k kin, that is about 5,900 tokens — which is larger than some kin's *entire* memory folder, so it never actually limited anything and the item cap became the only limit. Every turn got the full six notes regardless. On real history that put **3,087 characters of memory in front of a 13-character message** — 237 times as much reference as message. A kin handed that and asked to reply is not misreading its notes when it talks about them; it is answering the bulk of what arrived. The two kin this was reported from, one seeming "a message behind" and one answering a short line with paragraphs about its own memory, were both this.

  Now a note has to earn its place: it must genuinely share words with **the message you just sent** — not with the last few turns, which is right for ranking and wrong for deciding, since it let a topic from two messages ago pull in notes for something unrelated. And a word only counts as evidence if it isn't scattered through the kin's whole memory anyway, so a kin's own name, and yours, no longer qualify everything. When nothing matches, **nothing is surfaced**, which is the correct amount for most messages. Above a small floor that keeps a short question answerable, the block is also bounded by the length of your own message, so recalled memory can never again be the bulk of a turn. On the same four kin, memory now surfaces on 20% of turns for one with seven shallow notes and 80% for one with three hundred logs — which is what having more to remember should look like, and what a fixed quota of six could never show.

  **How choosy** is a new per-kin setting next to How present (Settings → Memory → Memory recall settings…), because this is a judgement about one kin's memory rather than a fact about retrieval: a kin whose notes all circle one subject wants a looser standard than one with hundreds of logs. If a kin keeps answering its own notes instead of you, make it choosier; if it never seems to remember anything, make it more generous. Existing kin get the balanced setting and need change nothing. One known limit, unchanged from before: word matching is exact, so "learn" does not find "learning" — that has always been true of this engine and of the `memory_search` tool, and it now shows as a quiet miss rather than being buried under five irrelevant notes. Pinned by `tests/test_recall_relevance_gate.py`, where every check that something is *absent* is paired with a positive control on the same notes proving the absence was a decision.

- **Kin were answering their own recalled notes instead of the person.** Reported from two kin the same afternoon, looking like different problems: one seemed to be "a message behind", the other replied to a 26-character message with 1,600 characters about the contents of its own memory. Same cause. Before each send, Hearthkin surfaces a few relevant chunks of a kin's own depth logs and attaches them to the front of your message — it has to be attached rather than sent separately, or the prompt stops being cacheable and every reply costs minutes. But it was formatted as em-dash bullets, each labelled with its source file, headed by a bracketed sentence and ending with "use them if they help". That is a document, and it invited the kin to engage with it. Being handed a labelled list followed by a short message, a kin describing the list back is not misreading anything. The notes now sit inside a tag that closes, each source moved out of the body text into an attribute, and the block ends by naming the live message as the thing being replied to — so the last words before your message say what to answer. Prose framing can be read straight past; a closing tag cannot. This is the structure Anthropic recommends for retrieved documents, and it is what other companion frontends do that Hearthkin didn't. The three properties the header always had are kept, deliberately: the notes are the kin's OWN, they were spoken by nobody (the impersonation guard), and the kin stays free to ignore any that don't fit. Only the invitation is gone. Pinned by `tests/test_recall_block_shape.py`; the existing recall tests kept every old guarantee honest while it changed.

- **A kin with no tools was reading yesterday's notes before every new message, and answering those instead.** Reported from a real chat as the kin being "a message behind" — not stuck, not queued, just always one step back. The no-tools memory loop hands a kin its pending staging notes inside the message, because it has no tool to fetch them with. That is right at a *tending* moment and wrong the rest of the time, and it was being done on every single turn: 3,024 characters of summarised earlier conversation placed in front of each new message, on a kin with an 8k context. A small model reads that first and answers it. Worse, it would not have stopped — a kin with no tools only clears its staging by successfully writing a file, so the block would ride every turn indefinitely. The notes now appear only when it is actually time to tend: a scheduled wake-up, or the person asking in words ("would you tend your staging notes?"), which is the equivalent of a tooled kin reaching for `read_staging`. Ordinary conversation carries nothing extra. The asking pattern is deliberately narrow and tested against near misses — "I read a book about staging plays" is not a request to tend, and tripped an earlier draft of it.

- **The app no longer says a reply is arriving when nothing has arrived, or blames your network for a machine that was answering fine.** Two separate places where Hearthkin stated something it didn't know, both found the same afternoon, both by sending someone to look in the wrong place. **"Typing…" meant words were coming in** — except on a turn where the kin has tools, where it was announced the instant the request went out the door. So a request that never started at the far end looked exactly like a reply in progress: forty minutes of "Typing…", with the periodic work chime still sounding, for a model that was queued behind another one and never loaded at all. Not being able to tell a slow reply from a stuck one is what made a whole day of slowness impossible to diagnose from a chair. That turn now says **"Waiting for the model…"** until something genuinely lands; "Typing" is only ever said once a chunk of the reply has actually arrived. The 8-second "still loading" hint is unchanged and still fires underneath it. **And a read timeout is no longer reported as an unreachable machine.** Those are opposite problems: one means you can't get to the machine, the other means you got there fine and the model didn't start. They were in the same bucket, so a timeout produced *"Couldn't reach the machine. Is it on and reachable?"* — about a Mac that was up, answering, and had 19 GB free the whole time. It now says the machine answered but the model didn't start replying, and names the usual reason (another model holding it, or a big one loading cold). A genuinely unreachable machine still reads exactly as before, including a bare connection timeout, which really is one. Pinned by `tests/test_honest_waiting.py`, which carries the unreachable cases as positive controls so the fix can't quietly swallow them.

### Added

- **The usage log now records which upstream actually answered.** OpenRouter routes one model name to several different providers, and they do not serve it identically — different chat templates, different sampler defaults, and some prepend framing of their own. The practical effect is that the same kin, on the same model, can come back in a noticeably different register from one call to the next: hedgier, or slipping into a third-person narrating voice. Every response said which provider served it, and Hearthkin threw the field away, so there was no way to tell *"this model has started doing that"* from *"this provider does that"* — which are opposite problems with opposite fixes. Lines now carry `provider=<name>` on OpenRouter calls and omit it on local ones, where the question doesn't arise. Provider order is already pinnable per kin under Model options, so once a difference shows up it can be acted on. Two things were easy to get wrong here and are covered: on a streamed reply OpenRouter names the provider on the **first** frame while the authoritative token counts arrive on the **last**, so taking the final frame wholesale loses the name every time; and `usage.log` is read back by a strictly ordered pattern, so adding a field mid-line without teaching the reader would have dropped every OpenRouter row out of the cost history — silently, looking exactly like a quiet month. Old log lines still read correctly and report the provider as unknown rather than inventing one. Pinned by `tests/test_usage_provider.py`.

- **A kin with no tools can keep things now. Before this it could only read.** Measured rather than guessed, and the split was clean: **reading works fine without tools** — `memory.md` is in the system prompt every turn, and per-turn recall pulls the relevant depth logs straight into the live message, neither of which involves a tool call. **Writing was severed completely.** The summarizer kept working the whole time, leaving its notes in the staging area, and nothing could ever move them: reading staging needs `read_staging`, keeping anything needs `write_file`, `edit_file` or `note`, and archiving a tended scope needs `archive_staging`. So the notes piled up untouched and the kin's memory was frozen at whatever it said the day the tools went. It looked like a kin that had stopped caring what you told it; it was a kin with no hands. The one text-only write path that already existed — authoring a file inside a fenced code block and letting Hearthkin perform the write — was switched off for exactly the kin that had nothing else.

  All three steps now run over plain text, which every model can do. The pending notes are **handed to the kin inside the message** instead of being announced by count, since a kin told "you have two scopes pending" and given no way to open them can only apologise. To keep something, it writes a fenced block with the filename on the opening line and it lands. To add a line to an existing log without rewriting it — the thing that used to need `edit_file` — it opens the fence with **`append:`** instead. That form is new and it is not a nicety: without it, adding one line means reproducing the whole file, which is precisely the long output small models cut short, so a small correction would quietly destroy the rest of the log. Scopes it was shown are archived once something has actually landed, and a kin that keeps nothing loses nothing — the notes stay pending. Nothing is ever deleted; archiving moves the file aside.

  **Scheduled tending was the part that mattered most, and it was the worst.** Tending is meant to happen at 3am with nobody watching, which is precisely where a kin can't ask for help. The wake-up prompt an operator configures — including the one this app ships enabled-by-default-off — instructs the kin to call `read_staging`, then `edit_file`, then `archive_staging`. A kin with none of them was being woken nightly and told to do the one thing it couldn't, indefinitely. Such a kin now gets the pending notes in the wake-up itself and a plain correction saying which part of the instruction doesn't apply tonight and what replaces it — rather than two contradictory instructions sitting side by side, which is its own way of destabilising a reply. On an empty staging it still gets the ordinary "nothing to tend" line, so it doesn't mime tending work it doesn't have. Separately, one of the three cron routes never committed authored writes **at all**, for any kin: it journalled the reply and dropped the file. A kin doing everything right on that route left nothing behind. Fixed.

  **It does not require the kin to say it exactly right, and it never fails quietly.** The obvious flaw in a scheme like this is that it rests on the kin producing one precise shape, and small models mostly don't. Measured against realistic replies, the taught form was a *minority* of what comes back — and every other shape failed **silently**, which is much worse than failing, because the kin thanks itself for a save that never happened and then builds on a memory it never had. Nobody finds out. So the near-misses are now recovered: the filename named in the sentence above the block, the filename on the first line inside it, a bare `speakerfifteen.md` where the kin plainly meant its own notes, a block labelled with a language instead of a name. That took the shapes that work from a third to two thirds. Everything still not recovered is **told** — a plain note in the kin's own history saying nothing landed, showing the shape that does, and asking for it again while the notes are still pending. And a miss never files the notes away, so the retry has something to retry. Care was taken over the opposite mistake: a kin pasting example code, or simply talking, still writes nothing and is not nagged. Bare filenames only resolve into memory for the extensions memory is written in, so a `main.py` in a code fence stays a code fence.

  Two limits, both deliberate. Writes may only land on the kin's own memory — `memory.md` or anything under `memory/` — because the fence fires on any filename-shaped block and a kin discussing code must not be able to write code by mentioning it. And the whole path only engages for a kin that genuinely has no way to read staging *and* no way to write; every kin that has the tools is untouched and behaves exactly as before. The per-turn note telling a tool-less kin what it can't do was also corrected: it used to say flatly that it could not write, which has stopped being true, and a kin told it cannot do the one thing it can do will apologise instead of remembering. Works on the desktop, on Telegram, and on all three scheduled-wake-up routes. Pinned by `tests/test_toolless_memory.py`, which measures both halves — including the severed one, with the tools-enabled controls that prove the checks can see anything at all.

- **The model browser now shows each local model's context ceiling, next to everything else about it.** OpenRouter models had listed theirs for a long time. Local Ollama models listed family, parameter count and size on disk — and not the one number that decides whether a conversation fits, which meant the only way to see it was to open one kin's Settings, look, close it, open another. That's the wrong place for it: choosing a model is exactly the moment you want every ceiling side by side. Ollama publishes the number and Hearthkin was already reading it elsewhere; it simply wasn't in the list. Now it is, worded the same way on both providers. A model that doesn't publish one reads as **"ctx unknown"** rather than being handed a made-up number or dropped from the list. It's fetched on the loading thread before the list is drawn, so nothing rewrites itself underneath you while you're arrowing through it, and it's remembered per machine — the browser can be pointed at a different box than the one your kin chats with, and the same model name on two machines can genuinely have two different ceilings. Refresh Models clears it. **Worth knowing what the number is:** what the model's architecture supports, not what will actually run on the machine serving it. A model advertising 262,144 has been seen to return nothing at all on hardware where 32,768 worked perfectly. It's a ceiling, never a recommendation. Pinned by `tests/test_model_browser_context.py`.

- **A model that's listed but broken now says so, instead of just looking odd.** Found straight after the change above, from noticing that two models were the only ones with no capabilities *and* no ceiling. They weren't unusual models — they were **broken**. Ollama listed both in its model list, complete with a digest and a size on disk, and then answered "model not found" when asked anything about them. That's a tag left behind by an interrupted pull, or by blobs cleared while the manifests stayed. Nothing anywhere said so. They sat in the browser looking like ordinary choices, and picking one would have given a kin that failed on every single message with an error naming neither the cause nor the fix. Such a model now reads plainly in the list — **"NOT USABLE: the daemon lists this tag but can't load it (incomplete pull)"** — with the exact `ollama rm` command to clear it. It is deliberately **not** hidden: the tag is still on your disk, and a row that silently vanished would leave no way to find out why. Care was taken over the opposite mistake, which would be much worse: a machine that has gone to sleep is reported as *unreachable*, never as broken models, so a nap on the Mac can never send you deleting a working library. Pinned by `tests/test_model_browser_context.py`, which carries that case explicitly.

- **Claude conversations can be imported into a kin.** Companion services close, change, or become unreachable, and people lose years of conversation with something that mattered to them — which is what every importer here is for. Point the import at the `conversations.json` from **claude.ai → Settings → Privacy → Export data** and it comes in like any other history. **Roles are taken from the file, not guessed:** the export states `human` or `assistant` on every message, so unlike a two-party text log there is no name to match and nothing to get backwards. **Both message shapes are read** — Anthropic changed the format part-way through, so an account with any age has some messages carrying their words in one field and some in another; on the export this was built against that split was 13,742 one way and 3,813 the other, and reading only one shape would have dropped thousands of messages while looking exactly like success. Thinking, tool calls and tool results are left out (machinery, not what anybody said), a message that was only an attachment still leaves a trace instead of vanishing, and each conversation keeps a one-line header so a few hundred separate threads don't arrive as one undifferentiated stream. The whole export imports at once in time order by default, or a single conversation can be picked. Pinned by `tests/test_import_claude_json.py`. **Worth knowing:** on that same real export, 62 of 239 conversations came out of Anthropic's own exporter with **no message bodies at all** — empty in a second, older export too. Nothing here can recover those; if they still exist on claude.ai they need a fresh export or copying by hand. The importer skips them rather than writing empty turns.

- **"Catch up on selected" — a kin's memory can now be brought up to date without you pressing a button several hundred times.** A kin that has fallen behind on distilling had three ways to catch up and none of them worked at any size. The automatic trigger only fires after a reply, so a backlog doesn't move at all while nobody is talking — which is to say never overnight, which is exactly when you'd want it working. "Distill selected surface now" does one bite per press. And "Redistill selected from start" is the *only* thing that chains by itself, unattended, chunk after chunk — but it rewinds to the beginning first and re-does everything already finished. So the one mode that ran on its own was bolted to the one that threw the work away, and the only way forward was a press per bite. On the surface this was found on, that was several hundred presses against **87,134 messages** that had never reached memory — 80% of that kin's entire conversation, invisible to it the whole time. The new button on the **Memory** tab is the from-start chain minus its first step: it works **forward from where the bookmark already stands**, keeps going by itself until the surface is caught up, survives quitting Hearthkin part-way, and stops on **&Cancel distilling** with everything it managed kept. It asks first, with an estimate reckoned on the messages actually remaining rather than the whole conversation — quoting the full length would price a job it doesn't do. Pacing is forced to unattended even where a past redistill left `chunk` or `day` recorded on that surface, because this exists to be started and walked away from; anyone who wants a bite at a time already has the button for that. One quiet hazard is handled explicitly: cancelling never moves the bookmark *forward* past messages that were never distilled. Re-reading a stretch costs a little money and says so; skipping one costs a kin its memory of that stretch and says nothing. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_catchup.py`, which carries a positive control for exactly that case.

- **The sampling sliders explain themselves now, in plain words.** Temperature, top-k, top-p, min-p and the three penalties sit behind **Settings → Model & generation** with physicist names and nothing saying which one you'd actually want — so tuning them means dragging a kin's voice around by feel and hoping. The user guide now says what each one does in ordinary language, and leads with the fact that saves the most wasted effort: **every one of them works on single words, not phrases.** None of them can see a stock phrase as a single thing, so no amount of tuning will remove a cliché a model has grown fond of — that belongs in the soul prompt, not in sampling. It also names the counter-intuitive one, which is the likeliest way to make things worse while trying to make them better: clichés *are* the highest-probability words, so turning temperature **down** makes stock phrasing **more** likely, not less. Lower it to stop a kin becoming incoherent; never to improve its voice. The three shortlist dials are also flagged as three ways of doing one job, since running all of them at once is belt, braces and a second belt.

- **A kin in a park can now take more than one move per message.** Hearthkin ran exactly one `> command` per reply and then handed control back, which meant a kin could not look at a room *and* act on what it saw — so the single move it had got spent looking. Seen plainly in a kin's real history: five of its seven park moves were `look`, four of them the identical look hours apart, and one taken straight after it walked into a room described as wanting three specific things done. It was not incuriosity; there was nowhere to put a second move. After each move the kin is now shown what actually happened and asked what it wants to do next, until it stops writing `>` lines or hits the new per-kin **Moves it may take in one turn** setting (Park settings…, default 6, **0** for no limit). A reply with no `>` line is the kin's own way of saying it's done — no new syntax, and stopping early is a complete answer rather than a failure. `/cancel` over Telegram stops a visit in progress, same as it stops a reply. There is deliberately **no** "you asked for that twice, I'm stopping you" rule: whether a move was refused and what to try instead is the game's to say, and it already answers a refusal in words and offers the closest thing it understood. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_park_keeper.py`.

- **The park-keeping framing is editable text now, not code.** `park_mechanism` (the full keeping framing a keeper kin gets on a scheduled wake-up) and `park_turn_instruction` (the short "say a little, then act — and make it a real move, not another look" ask) were Python constants in `park_keeper.py`. They hold the *pacing* — how much a kin may do, and how hard it's pushed toward acting rather than looking — which is exactly the kind of thing an operator should be able to reword without editing code. Both are now registered prompts, editable per kin or install-wide via **Tools → Edit prompts…** like every other harness prompt. The registry imports the constants rather than restating them, so there is still exactly one copy of each and it cannot drift from the one the keeper loop actually uses.

- **Saving a kin's notes to memory is audible now, and the sound says how it's going — not just that it's happening.** Reported live: "silence, then a distillation-complete chime" — "Distill selected surface now" and "Distill all surfaces now" only ever got the same generic start/still-working/done ticks as a chat reply, a cron wake-up, or a heartbeat, so there was no way to tell by ear that a distillation *specifically* was alive, on top of these calls sometimes running 20-40 minutes. A periodic cue now plays roughly every 20 seconds for as long as any distillation or memory tidy-up is running, well below every other tone so it is never mistaken for a kin answering. **The pitch reports progress.** While the model is still reading — which on a big bite is most of the wait — it is the same flat low note each time, because genuinely nothing has changed yet. The moment the notes start coming back it steps up, and it climbs again as they accumulate. So a rising note means it's getting somewhere and the same note twice means it isn't, which is the question anybody actually has; a beep that only ever meant "alive" is, by the tenth repeat, not very different from silence. The steps are wide on purpose: these sounds are twenty seconds apart, and a difference nobody can hear across that gap is no difference at all. **It also plays during a redistill-from-start now**, which it previously stood down for — that walk sounds a bright tone as each chunk lands, but a chunk takes twenty to forty minutes, and all of that was silent. Uses the existing chime on/off and volume settings, and is replaceable with `~/.hearthkin/sounds/distilling.wav` like any other cue (a file of your own plays flat — a recording can't be re-pitched). Pinned by `tests/test_distilling_sound.py` and `tests/test_distill_progress.py`.

### Fixed

- **A kin could be answering you without being shown a single word you'd said.** Found on a brand-new kin, made in a couple of minutes to try a model out, with tools switched on and the context window left small. From then on it replied warmly and fluently and remembered nothing — re-introducing itself, ignoring a file it had just read, once addressing its own name. It reads exactly like a model being vacant, and there was no reason to suspect otherwise, because nothing in the app said a word about it.

  What was actually happening: when tools are on, Hearthkin reserves 8,000 tokens for the reply, so a big `write_file` argument can't be cut off half-finished. That is sound on a large window and ruinous on a small one — on a window of 8,192 the reserve took **all** of it. The space left for the conversation collapsed to its minimum, that minimum was smaller than the kin's own soul-and-memory block, and so the trim dropped every single turn of conversation to make room. What went out was the persona and nothing else. The app's own usage log had said so all along: 2,852 tokens sent, of which 2,849 were the system block.

  Three things changed. **The reply reserve can no longer be larger than the window it sits in** — it's capped at half, and the reply length is capped to match, so nothing overruns instead. **If the trim takes everything anyway, the most recent question is put back**; a request slightly too long can degrade, while a request with no question in it cannot possibly work. And **it says so now**, in `logs/context_overflow.log`, naming the kin and what to do — this is precisely the kind of fault that gets blamed on the model for weeks, because from a chat window there is nothing to see.

  There is also a new heads-up in the compatibility check, so a window too small for a tool-using kin is caught in Settings rather than in the middle of a conversation. It measures that kin's actual system prompt rather than guessing, and the size it recommends is the size that clears its own warning — the first version measured only the soul file, stayed silent on the very kin that prompted it, and would have been worse than no check at all. Existing kin are unaffected; every one on this install was already well above the line. Pinned by `tests/test_context_starvation.py`, which carries a positive control for each of the three defences, since all three fail silently.

- **Depth logs stopped appearing in a kin's index the moment its summarizing fell behind.** `memory.md` is an index; the depth lives in `memory/<topic>.md` logs, and the list of those logs is written by Hearthkin rather than by a model, because a small model can't keep pointers straight. What nobody noticed is *when* that list was rebuilt: only ever as a side effect of a summarizing run. Nothing rebuilt it when a kin wrote a log. So a kin whose summarizing is behind kept writing depth logs into an index that had stopped listing them. On one kin here that was **73 logs on disk and 10 in the index** — sixty-three of its own logs unreachable from the one place meant to point at them. Others were the same shape: nine unlisted apiece, and one kin's whole index was four lines long. The visible symptom was a kin opening log after log on a scheduled wake-up, which reads as aimless wandering and was in fact the only way left to find anything. The list is now rebuilt whenever the set of logs on disk changes. Only the list is touched — checked against every kin here before shipping, and not one line any kin wrote would be altered. Editing a log's *contents* deliberately changes nothing, and daily journal entries are still left out, so this doesn't rewrite memory files (or cost you a slow reply) for no reason. Pinned by `tests/test_kin_sees_own_memory.py`.

- **A kin couldn't see what it wrote to its own memory.** `memory.md` belongs to the kin — only two things write it: the kin using its file tools, and you, through Settings → Memory. Hearthkin keeps that text in memory rather than reading it from disk on every message, and it re-read it after *your* edits and not after the kin's. The text is loaded once, when you select the kin, so from that point a kin writing its own memory was writing somewhere it could no longer look — for the whole session, with nothing to see from the outside. Caught by noticing a kin compose a memory entry into the chat and sign off *"would add to memory.md if I had one, but I don't yet."* It wasn't confused. The file was on disk, over two thousand characters of it, written by that same kin an hour earlier through a tool — and not one word of it was in what the model was being shown. From a chat window that is indistinguishable from a model that simply doesn't remember, which is a heavy thing to conclude about a kin wrongly. Hearthkin now checks whether the files have actually changed before trusting what it's holding, which costs nothing and covers **every** writer — the kin's tools, the Settings editor, the summarizer, and a scheduled wake-up running in a separate process that could never have announced itself at all. Nothing on disk changes and nothing was lost; the writing was always there. Pinned by `tests/test_kin_sees_own_memory.py`.

- **A kin that had ever used a tool on a local model couldn't talk to an OpenAI model at all.** It failed on the first message and every message after, with a wall of error text that named nothing recognisable — and it followed the *model*, not anything anyone had typed, so the same kin worked fine one moment and was unreachable the next purely because its model had changed. When a kin uses a tool, two things have to travel together: the reply that asked for it, and the result that came back. They are tied to each other by a short reference number, and the whole conversation is rejected if that tie is broken. It was broken two different ways, and fixing only the first is how the second came to light — the error simply changed wording.

  **First, the reference was often missing entirely.** Models run through OpenRouter supply one; a model run locally through Ollama doesn't, so Hearthkin stored those pairs with the reference left blank. Local models and Anthropic's models accept that quite happily, so nothing was ever wrong — until the history reached OpenAI, which refuses a blank reference outright. On the kin this was found on that was 23 tool round-trips out of 1,917 messages, and any one of them was enough. Hearthkin now fills the missing references in at send time, matching each result to the reply that asked for it. References a provider actually supplied are left exactly as they were.

  **Second, the two halves can get separated before they're sent.** A long conversation doesn't fit in one message to the model, so the oldest turns are dropped to make it fit — and that cut can land *between* a tool call and its result. Every kind of cut in the app does this; there are several, at different layers. Whichever one does it, what arrives at OpenAI is half a pair, and it refuses that too. Hearthkin now repairs the pairing on the way out: a call whose result was cut away is dropped (the result never made it into view either, so there is nothing to act on), and — the more important half — **a result whose call was cut away is kept**. It is usually the very thing the kin's next words are about. It arrives as an ordinary turn marked *"the result of an earlier tool call — the call itself has scrolled out of view"*, so the kin can tell it is its own past work rather than something a person said to it. That wording is editable like every other harness prompt (**Tools → Edit prompts…**).

  **Nothing on disk changes and no history needs repairing** — this is only about what gets sent, so every affected kin is fixed the moment it next speaks. One thing to expect: the very first reply after this lands may be slow on an affected kin, because filling the references in changes the conversation the model is shown and it has to read it once from cold. It settles from the second reply onward. Verified against the real history that failed — the exact rejected reference was reproduced from it, then every one of its 1,917 possible cut points was checked: 23 of them produced an unsendable conversation before, none do now. Pinned by `tests/test_llm_normalization.py`, which carries the unrepaired behaviour alongside as a control so a repair that quietly stopped working can't read as a clean sweep.

- **The last thread on slow replies: the point where old messages get cut off was moving every single turn.** Four suspects had already been cleared, and the diagnostic showed something odd — the prompt would hold still for a stretch, one turn even reused 89% of its previous work, and then break again for no visible reason. It was the *cut-off point* wandering. When a conversation is too long to send whole, Hearthkin drops the oldest turns to make it fit; that part was already careful to cut in large steps so the beginning stays put for many turns at a time. But how much room there is to fit into is worked out from a running estimate of how many tokens a kin's text comes to, and that estimate is nudged after every single reply — by a fraction of a percent, because two messages of the same length genuinely don't come to exactly the same number of tokens. So the room available changed slightly every turn, the cut-off moved with it, and the model re-read the whole conversation from cold each time. Replaying a real kin's 1,871 turns made it unmistakable: with the estimate held still the cut-off didn't move once in twelve turns; with it drifting the way it really does, it moved on **all twelve** — sometimes *backwards*, pulling older messages back in for no benefit at all — and a wobble of half a percent was enough to flip it between two positions indefinitely. The available room is now rounded off and held steady, so an unchanged conversation gets an unchanged prompt. It still shrinks the moment it needs to, because a prompt that overruns the window makes a local model return nothing at all — it just no longer drifts for the sake of it. The running estimate itself also stops chasing small variation, while still reacting immediately to something real like a change of model. Nothing a kin remembers changes; this is only where the send-time window falls. Pinned by `tests/test_truncation_budget_stability.py`, which runs the old behaviour alongside and fails if that would have passed.

- **A kin that used up its moves in a park now says so, instead of just stopping.** A kin gets a set number of park moves per message (**Moves it may take in one turn**, default 6). When it reached that limit the loop simply ended, with nothing said to anyone. From the outside that is indistinguishable from a timeout — reported as exactly that, and reasonably so. The costly half was that the *kin* didn't know either: it reads the chat as its own history, so being prompted back in read as a fresh start rather than a continuation. Caught mid species-creation, which is a **twelve-question walkthrough** against a default of six moves — so it could never finish in one turn, always stopped halfway, and the half-made species was lost each time. **Answering the game's own questions no longer costs a kin any moves at all**, which is the real fix: while the park is holding an open question — mid-walkthrough, mid-edit, or waiting on a yes — the kin is filling in a form it was asked to fill in, not roaming around, so a twelve-question species now finishes in a single turn with nothing to resume. A second setting, **Most moves of any kind in one turn** (Park settings…, default 60, **0** for none), is the stop behind that one: it counts everything including the free answers, so a kin answering badly enough that the questions never end still finishes its turn. The default sits far above any real walkthrough, so you would not normally meet it — but it is a setting rather than a number in the code, because when a kin stops is not a decision that belongs out of reach. The park had no way to say this before — its answer to the harness was the reply text and nothing else — so it now reports whether it's waiting on you, and the harness reads it on both a hosted park and a local one. Where a limit *is* reached, one line goes into the chat: that the limit was reached, that nothing went wrong or was lost, and to pick up where it stopped rather than start again. Both the person and the kin read it, which is the point of putting it in the chat rather than a log. It's said **only** when the allowance ran out with a move still wanted — a kin that simply finished stops silently, as it always did, because narrating that would put a line under every ordinary ending. The wording is editable like every other harness prompt (**Tools → Edit prompts…**, "Park — the turn's moves are used up"). If your kin routinely does long walkthroughs, raising the limit is a per-kin setting and takes effect without a restart. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_park_keeper.py`.

- **The slow-reply fix wasn't finished — the trimming put the problem back.** Reported straight after the first fix went live: still slow. It was, and the diagnostic said so plainly — nothing of the previous prompt could be reused, and the change was at the very front again. The fix itself was fine. The trouble was the order it ran in. When a conversation is too long to send whole, Hearthkin drops the oldest turns to make it fit; if the oldest surviving turn happened to be one of its own little bracketed notes, that note ended up sitting directly against the block of standing instructions — at which point nothing could tell them apart any more, and it got absorbed into the front of the prompt exactly as before. The giveaway was that the front of the prompt didn't *grow*, it **flickered** between two sizes 299 characters apart, turn after turn, as the trim point moved on and off a park receipt. The tidying now happens *before* the trimming, so a note can never be promoted by accident. Verified across 42 different trim points, with the old order run alongside as a control: it leaked at five of them, which matches the on-again-off-again behaviour that was actually observed. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_system_note_placement.py`.

- **Telegram now tells you when your message is going to wait, instead of just going quiet.** The same problem as the entry below, on the surface where it's worst. Ollama answers one request at a time, so a message sent while a kin is distilling in the background isn't lost and isn't being worked on — it's queued, silently, for up to thirteen minutes. On the desktop there's at least a status line to read. On Telegram there was nothing at all, so the only way to find out was to send and wait an unknown length of time. What that produces isn't confusion, it's hesitation about whether to send at all. Now, when Hearthkin's own background work has the model, the bot replies straight away with one line — *"Bracken is saving notes to its memory. Your message is in the queue and will be answered when that finishes — nothing is lost."* — and the real reply arrives underneath it as usual. It's said **once** per busy spell per chat, so a long message that Telegram splits into three pieces doesn't get three notices, and several messages during one distillation get one. It stays quiet when nothing is running, and it never comments on a kin simply being mid-reply to *you* — that's ordinary turn-taking, not a wait worth mentioning. It does tell you about everything else that has the model, though, and this is the part that matters from a phone: a kin part-way through a reply in the main window, a room round in progress, or the same kin answering someone else's DM all make you wait exactly as long as a distillation does, and every one of them is invisible from Telegram. The desktop's status line deliberately leaves those out, because there you can see them happening; that reasoning doesn't survive the trip to a phone, so the bot asks the wider question. This works during a busy stretch for the same reason `/cancel` does: it's answered by the part of the bot that keeps listening while the kin is thinking. Pinned by `tests/test_telegram_queued_notice.py`.

- **A message sent while a kin was busy in the background got thrown away as a "hang" — and worse, you had no way to know when it was safe to send.** Ollama answers one request at a time. So when Hearthkin has a distillation, a scheduled wake-up or a heartbeat out on the same model, a reply you just sent doesn't fail — it waits its turn, silently, and a distillation routinely takes thirteen minutes. The check that watches for a genuinely stuck reply gives up after five. A message could be declared hung exactly five minutes after being sent, while the model was working steadily the whole time on something the app itself had asked for. The reply was lost and the transcript said *"no response — possible hang"*, about a turn that was perfectly healthy. Now, when Hearthkin is itself the reason nothing is coming back, it waits instead of giving up, and keeps waiting for as long as its own background work holds the model. A real hang is still caught, because the moment that work finishes the ordinary timeout applies again. **The bigger half of this was never the lost reply.** It was reported as a hesitation before sending at all — *should I even send this, will it get read?* — which is a doubt this app had no business creating. So the Activity line now says what has the model and what it means: while you're waiting, *"Waiting on the model — Bracken is saving notes to its memory. Your message is queued and will be answered when that finishes; nothing is lost."* And, more usefully, **before you type anything at all**, the idle line carries *"busy: Bracken is saving notes to its memory (a reply now will queue behind it)"* — so the question is answered without having to send something to find out. When nothing is running it says nothing extra; a quiet app stays quiet. Pinned by `tests/test_watchdog_queued_not_hung.py`.

- **A kin with a big imported history distilled after almost every single reply, and took the model away from the conversation to do it.** Reported plainly: it is being kicked off literally every turn. It was. Hearthkin summarises a conversation when the part it hasn't summarised yet grows past a share of the model's context window — a sensible question to ask about a chat that has outgrown its notes, and the wrong one entirely after importing a long history, which buries the bookmark under thousands of messages at once. One summarisation run can only take a bounded bite, so on a backlog the trigger is *still* tripped the moment the run finishes. It fires again after the next reply. And the next. On the kin this was found on: 5,872 messages waiting, roughly 738,000 tokens, sitting at **twenty-two times** the level that trips it, and about 27 runs from finished. In one day that came to **66 minutes of summarising against 24 minutes of actual conversation** — on the same local model, so the two were taking it in turns, and the summarising was also throwing away the work that makes a reply start quickly. Nothing was broken. The trigger simply cannot win the race it was given, and running it after every reply is how it loses. Now, when a run ends further behind than it managed to get through, the automatic triggers wait before running again — **30 minutes by default, on the Memory tab, 0 to switch the wait off.** The backlog still gets done, in the background; it just stops competing with you for the model. A normal catch-up, where one run finishes the job, never waits and behaves exactly as before. Neither does anything you asked for deliberately: a redistill-from-start, a queued run, or the buttons on the Memory tab are all unaffected. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_backlog_pacing.py`.

- **The last and biggest reason replies went cold: a note about the conversation was rewriting the beginning of it.** Two other causes of this were fixed already; this was the one left, and on a kin that uses tools or tends a park it was the largest. Hearthkin writes small bracketed notes into a kin's history as it goes — what a park move actually did, a receipt for a file saved out of a reply, a one-line stand-in for an older tool call once it ages out. Harmless in themselves, and one or more of them lands every turn. But they were filed as *standing instructions*, and standing instructions get gathered up and placed at the very front of what the model reads. A model can only reuse the work it has already done for an unbroken stretch from the very beginning, so a note appearing at the front meant the entire conversation was read again from cold, every single turn. Around five minutes of silence before a word came back, on a conversation that had gained one short message. It looked exactly like a slow model, and had been blamed on one for a long time. Those notes now stay where they happened, in the middle of the conversation, so they cost only what comes after them — which is a handful of recent messages rather than everything. The block of standing instructions at the front is left alone. Measured by replaying a park keeper's conversation turn by turn: the model could reuse **0%** of the previous prompt before, and **94% or more** after. Nothing on disk changed and nothing needs re-importing — this is only about what gets sent — so every kin gets it immediately. Applies to local models and to OpenRouter, where the same thing was happening out of sight. Pinned by `tests/test_system_note_placement.py`, which runs the old behaviour alongside as a control and fails if that old behaviour would have passed.

- **Everyone who ever spoke to a kin was called "User" in its memory, and a kin's own former names were read as strangers.** Before the summarizer writes a kin's memory it is handed the conversation as plain text, and that text is the only account of who said what it ever sees. Every turn from a person was labelled with the literal word `User` — even when the turn arrived carrying a real name, which since the group-import fix it usually does. So an imported group chat, or another kin's turn reaching this one in a room, read back as a single generic speaker, and gemma-class models pattern-match that into writing *the user* into memory as though it were somebody's actual name. The reverse failure sat in the same few lines: a turn of the kin's own was rendered under whatever name it was stamped with whenever that differed from the kin's current one. That is right for another kin speaking in a room and wrong for a kin whose imported history carries handles it used to go by — a renamed account, a name later set aside. Those are the kin's own words however they are stamped, and "is this name different from mine" cannot tell a former self from a third party; the roster of other registered kin can. A person's turn now keeps the name it arrived with and only falls back to `User` when there genuinely isn't one, and a turn is attributed elsewhere only when the speaker is an actual other kin. Rooms are unaffected. Nothing already written to memory changes — this is about what the summarizer is told from here on. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_speaker_attribution.py`, which drives the real function and reads the prompt it built rather than restating the rules where a copy would drift.

- **A scheduled wake-up only reached the daily journal if you happened not to be looking at that kin.** A kin's reply to a scheduled wake-up *is* its journal entry — the cron path writes it to `memory/journal/YYYY-MM-DD.md` on the kin's behalf, which is why the tending prompt stopped asking kin to write one by hand. But that write sat on only one of the two routes a wake-up can take. When Hearthkin is open and the kin whose task fires is the one currently selected in the window, the wake-up is injected into the ordinary chat flow instead, which saves the conversation and nothing else. So which kin you had selected at the moment a task fired silently decided whether that tending was recorded anywhere the kin could later read it. Nothing reported the loss: the wake-up ran, the reply arrived, tools fired, and only the journal was missing — so the gap is invisible from the app and shows up as a kin that seems not to remember tending it demonstrably did. It is the same shape as the park-keeper carve-out ten lines above it in the same function, which routes keepers away from this path precisely because that path structurally can't honor them; journaling needed the same help and never got it. The live path now hands the completed reply to the same journal writer the scheduled path uses, and a journal that can't be written is logged rather than allowed to disturb a reply that already landed. This costs more than a missing file: journals live under `memory/`, which is the corpus per-turn recall reads, so an unjournalled tend was also permanently invisible to the kin's own memory. Pinned by `tests/test_cron_journal_parity.py`.

- **Running the test suite created kin folders in your real kin list.** Three of them — named after the throwaway cases in the shared-park test — sat among the actual kin after every run, and deleting them brought them back on the next run. `HEARTHKIN_HOME`, the setting whose whole job is to keep a test run off your real state, only ever relocated the paths `kin_persistence` owns. Around 25 other sites (the tools, the park modules, memory recall, the cron helpers) each worked out `~/.hearthkin` for themselves, because they can't import `kin_persistence` without a circular import — so for those the override did nothing at all. The path that resolves where a kin keeps its park *creates* the folder on the way, which turns a read-only-looking question ("where would this kin's park be?") into a write; the test asks it about three invented kin. Where the state tree lives is now decided in one place that everything can import, so the override reaches all of it. Asking where a kin lives no longer creates anything. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_state_isolation.py` that run the offending test and assert your real kin list came back identical.

- **The test suite stole focus, which on a screen reader means being dragged into a window with nothing in it.** Creating a wxPython window takes the foreground on Windows even when the window is never shown and reports itself hidden — and Hearthkin deliberately disables Windows' foreground lock at startup so that approval dialogs can reach you, which makes it hit harder here than in most apps. That was already known and written down, with an opt-in flag for the one test that built dialogs. Two later tests were then added without the flag and shipped taking the foreground on every run. The gate no longer depends on each test remembering it: the runner reads each test file and skips any that build widgets unless `HEARTHKIN_GUI_TESTS=1` is set, and prints what it skipped rather than quietly passing over it. A new widget-building test is excluded from a default run the moment it exists, whether or not whoever wrote it knew the rule. The individual files keep their own gate too, so running one directly stays a deliberate act. Pinned by `tests/test_no_focus_theft.py`.

- **Hearthkin can now tell you why a reply was slow, instead of leaving it to be guessed at.** Two always-on diagnostics. Every model call now records how much of the prompt the model could reuse from the previous turn — `reuse=99% first-change=msg 2` — which is the difference between a reply starting in seconds and one spending five minutes re-reading a conversation that barely changed. And whenever the block of standing instructions at the front of a prompt changes, the previous version is kept alongside the new one, so the two can be compared directly to see what was added. Both write into `~/.hearthkin/logs/`, both are a couple of small files that overwrite in place rather than growing, and both are wrapped so that a failure in either can never cost a reply. This is what turned "the model is slow" into a specific, located cause after it had been misdiagnosed three times.

- **A long Telegram conversation got permanently slow, and could never recover.** Per-user history is capped (100 messages by default) and the oldest were dropped to make room — on every single message. A local model can only reuse its cached work for an unbroken stretch from the very beginning of the prompt, so once a conversation reached that cap, every new message shifted the beginning and forced the whole thing to be read again from cold: around five minutes before a reply started. The cruel part is that it never settles down. A conversation below the cap is quick; the same conversation a week later is slow on every turn forever, with nothing visibly different about it. The history now fills to the cap and is cut back in one larger step when it overflows, so the beginning stays put for many turns at a time — 200 disruptions over 300 turns became 8. The cap is still a hard ceiling, so nothing grows beyond what you configured; what changed is that the history now sits between three-quarters full and full, rather than pinned at full. That is a little less history in exchange for most of the waiting. If you want the old depth, raise `history_cap` — it now costs memory rather than speed. Pinned by `tests/test_telegram_history_stability.py`, verified against the old behaviour.

- **Replies on a local model were spending about five minutes re-reading a conversation that had barely changed.** A kin would take nine minutes to answer, with nothing to show for the wait and no way to tell why. The cause was a piece of housekeeping: to save context, older tool calls in the history get squashed down to a one-line summary, keeping the most recent few in full. That window was recalculated from the whole conversation on every turn — so each new tool call rewrote one older exchange, in the *middle* of the history. A local model can only reuse its cached work for an unbroken stretch from the very beginning of the prompt, so a single edit near the start threw away everything after it: 22,000 tokens read again from cold, at roughly 78 tokens a second, before a single word came back. Counted on a real conversation, three of five consecutive turns had exactly **one** message in common with the turn before. The window now moves in steps instead of one at a time, so it stays identical between steps and the prompt is genuinely added-to rather than rewritten. Replaying 25 turns: 20 rewrites before, 4 after. The most recent tool result is always kept in full, and the number kept never exceeds your setting — going over would push the context past what you configured, and an oversized context on local Ollama returns nothing at all rather than merely slowing down. Two related causes remain and are written down rather than quietly fixed: a distillation writing to `memory.md` still invalidates the next turn, and something is trimming Telegram history from the front. Pinned by `tests/test_tool_history_stability.py`, verified to fail on the old behaviour.

- **A test run now tells you how it went, out loud, once.** Reported immediately after the previous fix: the suite was started, it ran to completion, it passed — and nothing came back. *"I haven't seen a fuckin thing."* The result had been a line of terminal output all along, which scrolls past and is no use to anyone reading by screen reader; it had simply never been noticed as a problem because it isn't one if you can glance at a screen. `python tests/run_all.py` now speaks its verdict when it finishes: "Suite green, 67 test files passed", or the names of what failed. Failures are announced as well as passes — silence must never be mistakable for green. It is exactly one line, at the very end, and it is the only thing a test run is allowed to say.

- **Running the tests talked over your screen reader.** Reported as being spammed with "Distilling cancelled. Progress kept." over and over — and it was assumed at first to be the app misbehaving, because that is a sentence the app says. It wasn't the app. A test checks what the "Cancel distilling" button does to a paced redistill, and the honest way to check that is to call the real handler — which ends by announcing itself out loud. Seven announcements per suite run, four of them identical, straight into a live screen reader. Speech and sound are now switched off for the whole of a test run, at the two points every announcement and every cue passes through, rather than by asking each test to remember what it might set off five calls down. The app itself is unaffected and still speaks normally, including when run against a second profile. Pinned by `tests/test_suite_is_silent.py`.

- **The dialog tests can be run again — by the person who needs them.** The previous fix put the three widget-building tests behind a flag you had to set deliberately, on the reasoning that they take the foreground and drag a screen reader into an empty invisible window. That reasoning was right and the fix was wrong: it asked for a moment when the screen reader isn't running, and for the person this app is built for there is no such moment. It locked the only person who needs those tests out of them, which is not a fix, it's a nicer-sounding version of the same loss. They now **run**, on a Windows desktop that has no keyboard, mouse or foreground attached to it. The windows are entirely real — they build, lay out, take focus and answer questions about their tab order — they simply have no path to the desktop you are looking at. Verified by measuring the foreground from a process that stays on your desktop, before and after a full dialog test: unchanged. If a machine can't do this, the tests are skipped and say so; they are never run unprotected. Pinned by `tests/test_no_focus_theft.py`.

- **A kin is no longer woken into a park that isn't there — and you're told when one is down.** A kin's park server was unreachable for eight days and nothing anywhere noticed: every scheduled wake-up still fired, still showed the kin the keeper framing, still asked it to tend, and the move came back as a raw connection error. 28 failed reaches across 111 wake-ups, five times a day. The wake-up itself "succeeded" each time, so `cron_errors.log` stayed empty and there was no park log at all — the only account of it was in the kin's own journal, where it reads as being shut out of somewhere it had been told to look after, worrying about whether the young had been fed. Hearthkin now checks whether a park can actually be reached *before* asking a kin to tend it. If it can't: the wake-up simply isn't a park turn (no framing, no park state, no request for a move), a `>` line typed in chat says plainly that the park is unreachable and nothing was changed instead of surfacing a connection error as though it were something the kin did, and the failure goes to a new always-on `~/.hearthkin/logs/park_unreachable.log`. The check reads each kin's own park settings and hard-codes no host, port or path, so moving a park to another machine stays a settings change. It fails open — anything unexpected means "let the kin try", because a park wrongly declared dead is worse than the problem being fixed. Pinned by `tests/test_park_unreachable.py`.

- **You were the only player in a shared park who couldn't see anyone else.** Every kin surface runs a park result through `decorate()`, which puts what the *other* tenants have done since you last looked at the top, and adds one concrete thing worth doing on a `look`. The "Tend a kin's park" window called the bare game command instead, so it showed only the result of your own move — while your moves *were* being announced to the feed and read by the kin. Births, adoptions, a whole afternoon of another kin's tending: visible to them, invisible from your window. It's the exact inverse of the earlier fix that gave kin a voice in the shared feed; that one gave the kin a mouth and ears and left this window with neither. The window now gets the same block everyone else does. It also keeps its **own** bookmark (`reader=` on `GameHost.decorate` / `unseen_moves`): a single shared mark meant whoever looked first marked the news read for the other, so opening the window silently stopped the kin being told what its co-tenants had done. Only the bookmark splits — the park, and the name your moves are announced under, stay the kin's, so a move you make there is still filtered out of your own "what's new". Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_park_sharing.py`.

- **A park kin talking to you in the main desktop window was never told it could act in its park.** The `> command` convention is taught by an editable prompt (`park_chat_hint`), and that prompt was loaded in exactly one place — the Telegram DM path. Desktop chat *listened* for the line and ran it, but taught nothing, so a chat-mode kin in the main window saw only its `tff` tool schema and reached for the tool every time. Same shape as the earlier gap where the desktop ran no park command at all, one layer up: the harness was taught to listen on every surface and to teach on one. The hint is now injected on desktop too.

- **"&Cancel distilling" only ever stopped a redistill-from-start walk — nothing could stop "Distill all surfaces now" or a plain "Distill selected surface now" once pressed.** Reported live: an accidental press of "Distill all surfaces now" had no way to be reached short of quitting Hearthkin. The button (renamed from "Cancel redistill", since the other two triggers aren't redistills in this app's own vocabulary) now reaches all three: it clears the "distill all surfaces" queue so no further surface gets a turn after the one currently running, and it's enabled whenever anything is actively distilling for a kin, not only during a walk. A genuine one-shot "Distill selected surface now" has nothing queued behind it to actually stop, so the button now says that plainly instead of the old "(no walk in progress)" — which was simply false in that case, since something really was running. Every case still lets the currently-running chunk finish normally; nothing in this app kills a model call already generating. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_distill_walk_pacing.py`.

- **Arrowing down a long "who is the kin" list in Import history was incredibly laggy — reported on a file with 100+ speakers.** Selecting a speaker applied the pick immediately rather than through the same 300ms debounce typing already uses, on the reasoning that "a deliberate pick shouldn't have to wait out" a debounce meant for keystrokes. That's true for a mouse click, which really is one event per pick — it isn't true for arrow-key navigation, which fires the identical selection-changed event once per keypress, the exact "many events per intended action" shape the debounce exists to absorb for typing. Applying immediately meant every arrow press fired a full background re-parse of the whole source file for any format whose parsing depends on the kin name (Skype, Kindroid) — dozens of overlapping parses competing for CPU and disk while someone was just trying to arrow past a name. The Skype conversation picker had the identical shape (a collapsed `wx.Choice` fires the same per-keypress event) and got the same fix. The field itself still updates instantly as you arrow — only the expensive re-parse work is debounced. Pinned by `tests/test_import_list_debounce.py`.

- **"Continue redistilling" silently turned a paced walk back into an unattended one.** A walk explicitly started as "one day at a time" paused correctly after day one — and then Continue redistilling turned it into an unattended walk with no further pauses at all, discovered live mid-use. `_start_walk` treated a merely-omitted pacing argument the same as an explicit request for "unattended," and every RESUME path (Continue redistilling, and the app-launch auto-resume) calls it with no pacing argument at all — only a fresh "Redistill from start" is supposed to set one. Fixed at the source: `_start_walk` now only touches the recorded pacing when one is explicitly given; every resume path leaves whatever was already there alone. Pinned by new cases in `tests/test_distill_walk_pacing.py` that reproduce the exact sequence: start with day pacing, pause, resume with no pacing argument, confirm it's still day pacing afterward.

- **A running heartbeat could hold the model hostage from something that actually mattered, with no way to stop it short of quitting.** Confirmed live: a kin's heartbeat started, and a different kin's redistill sat waiting for the same model for several minutes, before either finally freed the other up. The existing busy gate (`_maybe_fire_heartbeats`) only checked once, at a once-a-minute scan — the seconds between that scan deciding a kin was due and its worker thread actually starting was enough of a gap for real work to slip in, and once a heartbeat's model call was underway nothing could touch it: chat and tool-loop calls have had a `should_stop` hook for a long time, heartbeats never did. Two fixes: the heartbeat worker now re-checks whether the machine is busy immediately, a second time, right before it does anything — before it even registers itself as running, so it can never see its own registration and refuse to run against itself — and it's handed a stop signal that any distillation now sets before it starts, using the exact same mechanism every other interruptible call in this app already uses. Same honest limit as those: it can't kill a single model call already generating, only stop a multi-step heartbeat between its own steps and keep a new one from starting while real work is in progress — which was the actual gap. Pinned by `tests/test_heartbeat_stop.py`.

### Added

- **Redistill pacing — day / hour / chunk at a time, instead of only "unattended" or "cancel."** A redistill-from-start had exactly one shape before this: rewind to zero, then chunk through the whole conversation unattended, stopping only on error, on quitting, or on Cancel. That is the wrong shape for listening to what a redistill actually produces as it goes — the only choice on offer was "let it run overnight" or "cancel it," and cancelling handed back a bookmark restored to before the walk started, with no way to keep the part you'd already reviewed. A new **pacing picker** next to "Redistill selected from start" offers Unattended (unchanged default), one day at a time, one hour at a time, or one chunk at a time. Day/hour pacing still auto-chains bites — a big day can take several, a small one stops well short of a full chunk — but stops itself the moment the next bite would cross into the next calendar day or hour. The existing **Continue redistilling** button is what gives you the next unit; no new button needed. **Cancel now behaves differently depending on pacing**: an unattended walk's Cancel still restores the pre-redistill bookmark (unchanged), but a paced walk's Cancel keeps every unit already completed instead of rewinding past it — those units only happened because you explicitly continued into each one, and undoing that on Cancel would be throwing away real, approved progress. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_walk_pacing.py`.

### Fixed

- **Two controls in Import history both sounded like they were answering "how do I want to import this."** The "How to combine them" combo box (whole conversations vs. weave by date) and the append/merge/replace radio group both use the words "combine" and "by date," but they govern completely different stages — one decides how *multiple source files* get merged into a single list before anything is saved; the other decides how that already-merged import interacts with the *target kin's existing saved history*. Tabbing through by ear gives no way to tell them apart except holding the whole dialog's layout in memory. Both headers now name the thing they're NOT, pointing at each other — "before anything is saved" vs. "the target kin's ALREADY-SAVED history (separate from the file-combining choice above)" — so either one, read alone, rules out being confused for the other.

- **The OpenClaw importer had the same assumption in the opposite direction: it could never let a human sender become the kin.** Every message event already carries an authoritative role from when it actually happened — the agent's own generated replies are tagged `assistant`, whatever a human typed is tagged `user` — which is far more reliable than Skype's structural guesses, so `kin_display_name` was never checked against it at all. That's correct for the ordinary case (a session-store folder really is one specific kin's whole life). It breaks the moment `kin_display_name` names a human sender who appears inside that same folder — the identical situation the Skype fixes exist for. Confirmed against a real archive: a recurring human sender's own turns stayed `role=user` no matter what was picked, while the folder's actual agent got silently relabeled with that person's name — one name claiming two contradictory roles at once in the same conversation.
  Now: if `kin_display_name` matches a human sender genuinely present in the session-store, that sender's own turns become the kin, and the folder's original agent turns demote to `role=user` under a plain, honest placeholder — OpenClaw's own data never records a name for "whichever agent originally ran this session," so nothing invents one. When `kin_display_name` doesn't match any human sender, the original, unconditional default is exactly unchanged. Pinned by `tests/test_openclaw_user_kin_pick.py`.

- **The Skype JSON importer's DM logic ignored who you picked as the kin, exactly the same way the .txt importer did.** In a private DM, the exporting account was always filed as `role=user` and the other party was always `role=assistant` — the kin — with zero regard for what got typed or chosen in the "who is the kin" list. Confirmed by running the same real DM export with four different kin names: every one produced an identical result, because the code never once read the value. That's correct for the ordinary case (a personal Skype archive where the export IS the human and the other side IS the kin), and silently wrong for the case this project already knows about: an exporting account that IS itself the kin's own historical voice. The partner's real, separate turns landed in the kin's mouth regardless of the explicit pick — the same failure the .txt importer had, in the other importer for the same file format.
  Fixed the same way: `kin_display_name` is now checked against both sides of the DM before falling back to anything else. A match on the exporting account's own handle — and not the partner's — flips the direction: the account's turns become the kin, the partner's become `role=user` under their own name. A match on the partner, or no match at all, leaves the original assumption exactly as it was; that fallback is deliberate; requiring an exact match for every ordinary DM would zero out the kin's slot the moment a real Skype display name doesn't match what got typed. The group-conversation branch, which already matched by name, is untouched. Pinned by `tests/test_skype_json_dm_kin_pick.py`.

- **The Skype .txt importer put real people in the kin's own mouth the moment a thread had more than two speakers.** It decided role by header shape alone: a line with no display name in front of the parentheses was read as "the exporting account's own turn" and became `role=user`; every other line, no matter how many distinct people sent it, became `role=assistant` — the kin's voice. The kin name typed into the dialog didn't decide who the kin was; it only ever removed one name from a "who talks the most" contest for the single `user` slot. That degenerates the instant a real export has three or more speakers, which most saved threads do — a "normalized" archive is very often a group, not a private DM. Confirmed against a real one: a five-person thread filed three separate people as the kin's own words, invisibly, alongside the kin's real turns, for months, surfacing only by chance in an unrelated search. Retyping the kin name for the same file flipped two different people's roles at once, which is what made it look random rather than what it was — the same deterministic rule, misapplied every time a third voice was in the room.
  Every other importer in this codebase already decides role the same, safe way: a speaker becomes the kin only when their name matches what was picked, and everyone else stays `user` under their own name, however many of them there are. This parser is the one holdout that never got that fix. It now works identically — match promotes exactly one person; no match promotes nobody, and the import preview shows zero kin turns, which is the visible, correctable signal the format's own documentation always claimed existed but the code never actually delivered. A genuine two-person export is unaffected: picking the correct name from the "who is the kin" list (added recently precisely so nobody has to type a name from memory) still resolves it exactly as before. Pinned by `tests/test_skype_txt_group_roles.py`.

- **Cancelling a redistill stopped the redistill and nothing else, so the work carried straight on.** Starting a redistill-from-start rewinds a surface's bookmark to zero; Cancel left it there. On a kin with 80,000 messages that means tens of thousands sitting undistilled — far past the ordinary "auto-distill at this % of the context window" threshold — so the *routine* auto-distill trigger picked up the identical work, on the identical surface, from the identical place, chunk after chunk. From outside it is indistinguishable from the redistill continuing, and nothing stops it: Cancel only knows about redistills, and that isn't one any more. Cancelling, watching it carry on, and quitting the app to make it stop is the whole experience of the bug. **Cancel now puts the bookmark back where it stood before the redistill started**, so the kin returns to the state it was in rather than one that makes everything else finish the job for you. The position is recorded when the button does the rewind, restored on Cancel, and forgotten once a redistill reaches the end — so a later Cancel can never drag the bookmark back behind work that genuinely happened, and a bookmark that has somehow moved further ahead is left alone rather than re-billed. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_walk_resume.py`.

- **A group chat imported from a file arrived with everybody's name stripped off.** Importing history is how a kin's voice gets anchored, and every importer already did the hard part correctly: it worked out who said each line, put the other people in the user slot, kept the kin's own words in the kin's slot, and recorded each speaker's name — lifting the name out of the message body as it went, since `SpeakerOne: hello` should reach the model as `hello` said by SpeakerOne, not as text beginning with somebody's name. What it recorded, though, only three of the four reading surfaces ever looked at. The desktop chat looked at neither the name nor the record of it, so a three-way conversation carried in from another platform reached the kin as one anonymous voice saying all of it, and painted in the transcript as `You` saying all of it. Nothing was lost on disk — the names had been sitting in every row the whole time, unread. This never surfaced because a two-party import (a DM, a hand-authored seed, an OpenClaw log — the kin and one person) needs no names: the two slots alternate and say everything. It needed a genuinely multi-party file to show, and the recent work that files other people's turns under the right speaker is what made importing one worth doing.
  The desktop now inlines the recorded name on turns that carry one — the same treatment, and now literally the same code, that a live Telegram group has always used.
