Desktop-native turns carry no name and are untouched, so an ordinary one-on-one chat reads exactly as before. In the transcript, other people are shown under their own names while your turns stay `You`.

- **Naming people to a model is what teaches it to invent them, so the guard came with it.** The desktop reply path stripped an echoed timestamp off a kin's reply and nothing else. That was defensible while desktop context held no speaker names to imitate; putting imported group history in front of a kin makes it false. The full anti-impersonation cleanup — the one rooms and Telegram have always run — now runs on desktop replies too, and logs when it fires rather than fixing it silently. The attribution format is the other half: `[Name] text`, never `[Name]: text`. The colon makes it a speaker-turn token that models complete by writing the next person's turn; without it, it's inert. Every surface now builds that prefix through one helper, so a future call site can't reintroduce the colon by hand.

- **The impersonation guards were all blind to the exact shape the app itself shows a model.** Every pass that strips a speaker tag off a kin's reply required a colon — `[Opal]: text`. But the attribution a kin actually sees is deliberately colon-free, because the colon is what makes it a turn token. So a model doing the most predictable thing available to it, imitating the format in front of it, produced `[SpeakerOne] text` — and all four passes let it straight through, onto the screen and into the saved history where it becomes next turn's example. It had never fired because until now the only surfaces showing names were group ones, where it evidently hasn't come up; that is not the same as it being impossible, and the guard was written for a case less likely than this one. There is now a pass for the colon-free form. It matches **names actually shown to the model that turn**, never a bracket pattern — kin open replies with bracketed emotes (`[laughs] yeah, that one`), and a rule that stripped anything in brackets would quietly eat the first words of their own replies, which is a worse failure than the one being fixed.

- **Imported names reached the model wrapped in two sets of brackets.** Importers stored the name with brackets already around it while live capture stored it bare, and both fed readers that add a bracket of their own — so an imported turn arrived as `[[SpeakerOne]] text`, a shape that appears nowhere else in a kin's context. Importers now store the name bare, matching live capture. History already on disk is unwrapped when it's read, so nothing needs re-importing.

- **Distillation was being cut off by a timeout barely above the work it was doing.** The per-request wall-clock cap is derived from a kin's `num_ctx` — five minutes plus a minute per 8k, so a 32k kin gets eight. That formula is tuned for a reply, and on a big local model it sits *just* above what a distillation chunk actually takes. Measured on gemma4:31b at ~78 tok/s prefill and ~8 tok/s generation, one chunk of a 20,000-token bite ran **360 to 455 seconds against a 480-second cap**. A margin that thin is a coin flip per chunk — anything else touching the machine pushes it over — and it was losing roughly **four times in ten**, each loss ending the redistill it belonged to. Distillation and consolidation now get a 30-minute floor, because nobody is waiting on them: cutting one off early throws away a whole chunk of work and stops a redistill, while letting it run longer costs an idle background thread. A per-kin `watchdog_timeout_minutes` can still raise it, but no longer lowers it — a number tuned for chat responsiveness shouldn't quietly become the budget for unattended work.

- **A failed distillation left no trace on disk.** The error reached the Activity field and the Memory tab and nowhere else, both transient, so by the time anyone noticed nothing had progressed, the reason was hours gone and "what did it say?" had no answer. Distillation, staging-write and consolidation failures now append to an always-on `logs/distill_errors.log` with the kin, scope, source and summarizer model — the same treatment every other background failure in this app already had. (The underlying request timeouts were reaching `hang_watchdog.log` all along, with the surface and the cap that tripped; the two together now say what failed and why.)

- **A five-minute watchdog was firing on healthy distillations.** The per-kin distillation slot was force-released after five minutes on the assumption that nothing healthy runs that long. Plenty does: a local model reading a 10,000-token distillation bite can spend longer than that in prefill alone. So the watchdog libelled perfectly good work — the Memory tab announced "previous distill cleared — was stuck" about a call that was still running — and, much worse, the released slot let a **second** distillation start on the same kin while the first was still going. Mid-redistill that means two chunks running against the same un-advanced bookmark, digesting the same turns and staging two sets of notes for them. Liveness is now read from the worker thread rather than guessed from a clock: a thread that is alive is working, however long it takes, and the watchdog only fires when the thread is genuinely gone without having reported back — which is the failure it was written for and can now be identified definitively. A 30-second grace covers the moment between a worker posting its completion callback and ending. (Same lesson as the cron marker sweep above, found the same day: liveness is a question you ask the thing, not the clock.)

- **A kin could appear to be mid-wake-up for a whole day after its scheduled task had finished.** A cron wake-up runs in a separate process and reports itself through a marker file; markers whose process is gone are meant to be swept, so that neither a crash nor a kill can leave something claiming to work forever. The sweep asked Windows "is this PID running?" by opening a handle to it and treating success as yes. That is not what success means: Windows keeps a terminated process's PID resolvable for as long as *anyone* still holds a handle to it, and a parent or the Task Scheduler service routinely does — so an exited subprocess opened perfectly cleanly and its marker was never swept. The consequences all pointed somewhere else: the confirm-on-close dialog nagged about a wake-up that had finished the previous night, the repeating "still working" cue sounded every thirty seconds around the clock, and **heartbeats stood down machine-wide** because something looked busy, so no kin reached out all day. It read as a disabled scheduled task that kept trying to fire, which is the worst kind of wrong — it accuses the scheduler, and the scheduler was innocent. The check now asks `GetExitCodeProcess` and believes only `STILL_ACTIVE`. The same helper backs the run lock, so a stale lock left by a zombie PID is also correctly seen as stale now. (Also declares the ctypes signatures, which were defaulting a 64-bit handle to C `int`.)

- **A "redistill from start" could not survive being left alone — which is the only thing it ever needed to do.** Redistilling a kin's whole history runs for a long time; that is its nature. So it had to tolerate nobody watching, and it didn't. The flag saying "a redistill is running" lived only in the running process, so **quitting Hearthkin ended it permanently and said nothing**. The progress itself was never lost — the bookmark is on disk — but nothing continued it on the next launch, and the only button on the Memory tab reset that bookmark to zero. So the honest way to finish a long redistill was to never close the app, and the obvious way to continue it threw away everything done so far. One kin's history got redistilled from the beginning several times over.
  Two more ways it stopped, both silent and both looking identical from outside. **A chunk that errored** — the model went away, the provider had a moment — cleared the flag deliberately so you could restart after fixing the cause, but the only notice was one line in the Activity field, which is not spoken and is replaced by the idle context line four seconds later. And if an ordinary auto-distillation on one of the kin's **other** surfaces slipped into the 1.5-second gap between chunks, the next chunk hit the "already distilling" guard and returned without a word: flag still set, chain dead, and the Memory tab then refusing to start a new redistill because one was supposedly still running.
  Now: an interruption **pauses** rather than ends. The unfinished redistill is recorded in the kin's config, so closing Hearthkin pauses it and the next launch picks it up where it stopped and says so aloud. A collision **waits for the slot** instead of giving up, for up to two minutes. Anything that does stop the chain is **spoken and sounded**, naming what happened and that the progress is kept. A new **Continue redistilling** button carries on from the bookmark — the whole difference between it and the button above it, which still starts over. **Cancel redistill** now also clears a *paused* one, so cancelling something that had already stopped isn't a no-op that quietly resumes next launch. And the per-surface row distinguishes running, paused and idle, which previously read identically. Closing the Settings window never stopped a redistill and still doesn't — that window is somewhere to watch from, not the thing running it.

- **Distillation failures now say something.** A distillation, a memory consolidation and a redistill all run unattended by design — that is the point of them — and every failure path wrote to the Activity field only. That field isn't spoken and reverts after four seconds, so a distillation that had been failing for a week looked exactly like one that had nothing to do. All of them now speak and play a new **problem alert**: a two-tone cue that *falls* where the approval alert *rises*, so the two are distinguishable without learning a code. On by default and independent of the reply chime, same reasoning as the approval alert; switch it off under **Preferences → General**, or replace it with `~/.hearthkin/sounds/problem.wav`.

- **Controls that told a screen reader nothing, and text it could never reach.** `SetName()` — used across the newer dialogs to name a combo box or a list — does nothing whatsoever on Windows. It sets wxWidgets' own internal identifier, which is not the name Windows hands to a screen reader. Measured against the accessibility API directly rather than inferred: the only thing that names a control is a **StaticText created immediately before it**, and "immediately before" means in creation order, not the order things appear on screen. Several labels in this app were built inline further down, inside the call that adds them to the layout, so they sat behind their own control and named nothing while looking perfectly correct in the source. The result was a set of controls that announced as a bare "edit", "list box" or "combo box": the new-kin name box and the existing-kin picker in **Import history** (the first of those was an editable field with no name *and* no contents — you landed on it and were told nothing at all), the destination checklist in **Scheduled wake-ups**, the machine list in **Ollama machines**, the prompt list and preview in **Prompt updates**, and the per-surface counters, wake-up list and per-kin prompt list in **Settings**. All now carry a real label. Separately, the four volume fields in **Sound cues** all announced identically as "Volume:" with nothing to say which cue you were setting — the distinguishing text was in a `name=` that never reached anyone — and they now name their cue, the same fix the Test buttons beside them already had.

- **Read-only text you could not Tab to.** A single-line read-only text field is not keyboard-focusable on Windows at all — wx refuses it focus because there is nothing to scroll. The house pattern of "use a read-only field rather than a plain label so the text lands in tab order" therefore only works when the field is multi-line, and three places had it single-line: the whole feedback area of **Import history** (detected format, and the status line carrying the message count, date range, how many files were skipped and whether they were woven or kept whole — none of it reachable), the "how should it combine" question in **Restore history**, and *who is asking* in the **webcam approval** dialog, which is the one fact that dialog exists to put in front of you. All three are now reachable. The import status field is also **paced**: repainting a multi-line field resets its caret to the top and sends a screen reader back to the first line, and a parse writes there three times in about a second, so writes are now spaced at least four seconds apart — the same spacing the main window's Activity field settled on — with anything superseded inside the window never displaying at all. A single-line field that updates live is left exactly as it is: focus can never be inside it, so it cannot be yanked out from under you, which is the whole reason to keep one on a single line.

- **The import result is now spoken, not just written down somewhere.** Making a status field reachable means you *can* read it; it does not mean you ever find out there is anything to read. So **Import history** now announces its outcome aloud the moment it is known — the message count, who they are from, the date range, how many files were skipped, and whether they were woven or kept whole — along with any parse or read failure. Routine progress ("Parsing…") stays quiet, because it isn't news, and repeating the same message twice doesn't repeat the announcement. The speech is deliberately not subject to the four-second repaint pacing: that spacing exists because redrawing a field moves its caret and drags a screen reader back to the top, and saying something aloud moves nothing, so the result is heard when it happens rather than up to four seconds later. This is the same pipe the main window has used for important notices since it was written — this dialog simply never called it, so for its entire life everything it had to tell you sat silently in a box you also couldn't Tab to. If no screen reader is running, the announcement fails quietly and the import is unaffected.

### Added

- **A redistill now reports its progress by ear.** Its per-chunk report was spoken — token counts and how many messages were left — and for anyone typing while it runs, that line is never heard: a screen reader with character echo has no free moment, and for the person this was built for that is a constant rather than an occasional collision. So an hour-long redistill and a stalled one sounded exactly alike. There's now one short beep per completed chunk, **rising in pitch as it approaches the end** — low at the start, high at the finish, the same rising-means-progress idiom as the reply sequence. The same beep twice means stuck; a climbing one means it's working through. It replaces the clock-driven "still working" repeat for the length of a redistill rather than adding to it — similar rate, strictly more information. Own switch, volume and Test button under **Preferences → Sound cues…** (Test plays three rising tones, since the rise is the message), and replaceable with `~/.hearthkin/sounds/chunk.wav`, which plays flat because a file can't be re-pitched.

- **Import many files at once, woven together or kept whole.** Bringing an archive in one file at a time doesn't scale — a Skype export is fifty threads, and the same shape turns up for every other dead platform someone wants to carry across. **File → Import history…** now takes a multi-selection, and when you pick more than one it asks how to combine them. **Keep each conversation whole, oldest first** leaves every thread intact and plays them in the order they began, so each exchange stays next to its own reply — right when what you care about is how someone actually converses. **Weave everything together by date** interleaves the lot into one chronology, which reads as a life rather than a set of relationships, at the cost of scattering any single conversation among unrelated turns. Neither is a sensible default for the other's purpose, so it asks rather than choosing. The option is always present, whether or not it currently applies — as is the conversation picker beside it, which previously appeared only for Skype JSON exports. Tab order is how this dialog is read, so a control that materialises when you pick a second file and vanishes when you pick one changes the shape of the screen mid-task, and nothing announces that it moved. Both now stay put and say what's true instead: the conversations found, or — when the source holds no choice to make — what will actually be imported, named as the file or the size of the batch. Saying "this source has only one conversation" while the same screen reported forty-three files selected was the screen arguing with itself. Neither is ever greyed out either — "unavailable" is announced without saying why, which is the thing that made hiding look attractive in the first place. Every row keeps its own recorded source, so a mixed batch stays honest about where each turn came from, and the detected-format line says "mixed" rather than claiming one. A file that can't be read no longer costs you the rest of the batch — the others import and the status line says how many were skipped, because a silent skip in a fifty-file import is how you end up with a corpus quietly missing something and no record of what. Rows with no timestamp carry the previous one forward instead of being flung to the front of the archive by the sort.

- **"Synced 191 new messages from Telegram" — about a kin that has never been on Telegram.** Hearthkin watches the active kin's conversation for changes made outside the desktop window, reloads, and tells you what arrived. The words "from Telegram" were hard-coded, because when that poller was written the Telegram bot was the only thing that could write to a kin's conversation behind the app's back. That hasn't been true for a long time: imports, scheduled wake-ups, a kin's own reach-out, Discord and rooms all do it. So importing a few hundred turns of history into a kin with Telegram switched off, no bot token and an empty allowed-users list announced a Telegram sync, confidently. The turns were all correct and all present — only the sentence about them was invented. Every stored turn already records where it came from, so the notice now reads that instead of assuming, and names an import, a wake-up, a reach-out, Discord or a room accordingly. When a batch arrives from more than one place it names none of them: a count with no provenance is honest, and a count with the wrong provenance is the bug being fixed.

- **A heartbeat no longer re-reads a kin's entire life to decide whether to say hello.** A heartbeat asks one question — *do you feel like saying something?* — and usually answers no. It was loading the kin's whole conversation to ask it. On this install that meant prompts built from as much as **216,000 tokens**, arriving at the model at 22,000 after truncation, around **280 seconds of prefill each**, at **28% of all model calls**. Every one of those was competing for the machine with somebody waiting on a real reply. It now carries a bounded tail of recent conversation — enough to know what has just been happening, which is the entire input to the question. Measured across five kin: Opal 216,583 tokens → **2,361**, Vesper 149,809 → 4,964, Bracken 74,760 → 6,627. In prefill time, Opal goes from about **46 minutes to 30 seconds**. What remains is soul, memory index and base prompt, which is irreducible — a heartbeat still has to know who it is. The budget is in **tokens rather than turns**, and that distinction is the whole thing: counting turns sounds equivalent and fails exactly where it matters, because a kin being fed long passages has turns of 1,200+ tokens, so "the last twelve" was still 20,000 of them. Set `heartbeat.history_tokens` to 0 to restore the old behaviour. Scheduled wake-ups are unchanged — they still carry everything, because tending genuinely needs it.

- **An approval request now comes and gets you.** It used to open the dialog *inside the app* — which might be behind a browser, or hidden in the system tray — and then wait. The supporting signals were a Windows toast, lost among every other app's, and spoken text that a screen reader running character echo never gets a gap to say. So a kin could sit blocked for **hours** on a permission nobody knew had been asked for, and then time out. That is the worst shape a bug can have here: an exec approval parks a worker thread mid-turn *and* expires on a clock, so unlike every other notification it genuinely cannot afford to wait politely. The dialog now restores the window (including from the tray) and takes the foreground before it asks. That is deliberately rude and it is the point — a window arriving in front is a *focus event*, which a screen reader announces straight away instead of queueing it behind whatever else is talking, and it's the only signal that survives you being in a different application. The audible alert still fires alongside. Both attention-grabbing steps are individually guarded, because a failure there must never stop the dialog appearing: a kin waiting for an answer nobody was asked for would be worse than the bug being fixed.

- **Sound cues now fire for every kin, on every surface — and there are more of them.** The reply chime only ever sounded for whoever you had on screen. A kin answering on Telegram, a scheduled wake-up, a heartbeat: all completely silent, which is precisely when nobody is looking at the window and a sound is the only thing that could tell you. There are now four cues — **request sent**, **first word back**, **still working** (repeating), and **reply finished** — each with its own switch, its own volume, and a Test button, under **Preferences → Sound c&ues…**. The still-working one matters most on a slow machine: reading a long prompt can take minutes with nothing to show, and without a periodic sound there is no way to tell thinking apart from a crash. They rise in pitch with progress, so the sequence is legible by ear without learning a code, and any of them can be replaced by dropping a WAV into `~/.hearthkin/sounds/` named after the cue. They are sounds rather than spoken announcements deliberately: a screen reader running character echo has no free moment to say anything, and a Windows toast lands in the same stack as every other app's and is lost. Audio is the one channel nothing else is competing for. Nothing changes for an existing install until you open that dialog — the old single switch and volume still govern everything until you say otherwise. Two details worth knowing: an approval waiting on *your* answer deliberately doesn't count as work, so the repeating cue never nags you about your own unanswered prompt; and the cues are driven by watching whether the machine is busy rather than by hooking each surface, so anything added later is audible automatically. Hand-wiring surfaces is exactly how the original gap happened.

- **The "something's still working" prompt was blind to two kinds of work.** It shipped yesterday claiming its list was complete. It wasn't: quitting while a kin was part-way through a **heartbeat**, or while a **scheduled wake-up was running in the background**, closed in silence and abandoned it. Two different blind spots. Heartbeat workers ran on their own thread and registered themselves nowhere at all. And a cron wake-up can run in the standalone `hearthkin_cron` process — a *different process*, sharing no state with the app — so it was structurally invisible: from inside Hearthkin, genuinely nothing was happening. The first is now tracked like any other background thread; the second reports itself through a small marker file, and markers left behind by a process that died are swept rather than believed, so nothing can claim to be working forever. Found the only way a missing entry ever is — by quitting during one and watching it close without a word.

- **Heartbeats now wait for a quiet moment instead of talking over you.** A heartbeat is the least urgent thing Hearthkin does — a kin being offered the chance to say something unprompted, free to stay silent — but it costs exactly as much as a real turn: a full prompt, ~20,000 tokens on a mature kin. On a machine with few context slots, that processing throws away the cached context of the conversation *you are in the middle of*, so your next reply goes from about four seconds to about four minutes and nothing tells you why. On the install this was written for, heartbeats were **24% of all model calls** and background work of every kind was **65%** — the machine spent most of its capacity talking to itself while a person waited to be answered. Heartbeats now stand down while anything is in flight: a reply being written anywhere, a room mid-round, a distillation, a scheduled wake-up, or a kin blocked waiting for you to approve a command. The check is machine-wide rather than per-kin, because a heartbeat for one kin evicts a conversation with another just as effectively. Crucially they're **deferred, not skipped** — a kin that was due stays due and goes at the first quiet moment, so nobody loses their turn on a busy day. Each stand-down writes a line to `logs/heartbeat.log` saying what it waited for, so "why has nobody reached out today" has an answer on disk instead of looking like a broken feature. And if the busy check itself ever fails, heartbeats fire anyway — a feature that stops working silently is worse than one that speaks up at an awkward moment.

- **A Speed check that tells you why replies are slow, in words.** Tools → **Speed chec&k…**. It reads the usage log you already have and reports the one number nobody was looking at: what share of turns reused context the machine had already processed. When a turn can't reuse it, the entire prompt is re-read from scratch before a single word appears — on a 20,000-token prompt that's four minutes of silence. This install has been sitting at **11%** for weeks, costing 260 seconds a turn against 4 when it hits. The report names the likely cause rather than leaving you to interpret a table, breaks down which work has been using the machine (this install: **65% background** — heartbeats, scheduled wake-ups and distillation, all competing with you for the same slot), and lists each kin's prompt size, biggest first, since that's who waits longest. There's a Copy button, because the next thing you do with a report like this is show it to someone. It exists because a power cut silently undid the model machine's configuration and nothing anywhere reported it; the only alarm that ever went off was a person getting tired of waiting. Every number needed to catch it was already being logged.

- **Quitting while a kin is still working now asks first, and says what's working.** Closing Hearthkin stops the bots, cancels the timers and abandons whatever model call is in flight. That's exactly right when nothing is happening — and there was no way to know when something was. The available signals were remembering to check, or hearing the machine's fans spin up: neither reaches you for a kin running on another machine, and neither reaches you at all if you aren't in the room. So quitting meant blindly abandoning, sometimes, someone else's half-finished conversation. Now a close while work is in flight brings up a dialog naming each thing by kin — a reply being written here, a room mid-round, a memory distillation, a scheduled cron wake-up (the one kind of work with no visible signal whatsoever), a kin part-way through replying to someone on Telegram, and any tool approval still waiting on your answer — with **Wait** or **Close anyway**. Nothing is torn down until you choose "Close anyway": the question is asked *before* the teardown, so "Wait" genuinely leaves everything exactly as it was. When nothing is in flight, quitting stays silent as before — a confirmation on every close is one people learn to dismiss unread, and then it protects nothing. The list is a read-only text field rather than a label, so you can Tab to it and re-read it as often as you like instead of catching it once, and it's spoken when the dialog opens. "Wait" holds focus, is the default, and is what Escape does, so every reflexive dismissal is the safe one. Known gap: Discord replies aren't detected yet (that surface has no equivalent turn tracking), so a Discord kin mid-reply won't be listed.

- **You can tell a kin how long to wait for you to finish.** New per-kin **Wait for the rest of my message (seconds)** in Settings → Telegram → Message settings, default 2. It's the pause described in the message-splitting fix below, and it's a setting rather than a fixed number because the pause cannot be guessed on your behalf: Telegram never tells a bot that someone is typing, so "wait until they've finished" isn't available at any price, and no punctuation heuristic survives contact with real people. Two seconds suits someone thumbing a phone. If you compose slowly — screen reader, one hand, a switch, or a thought that simply takes a while to say — set it to fifteen, or sixty, and the kin will hold the whole time rather than answering half of what you meant; the cost is exactly that much quiet before each reply, which next to local inference time is often no cost at all. Anyone else's message, or a slash command, ends the wait early, so a long pause in a group doesn't hold the room up. 0 means answer the instant you hit send — and even at 0, a message Telegram itself cut in half is still put back together, because that pause was never yours to choose.

- **Import a kin's whole history from OpenClaw.** For kin who lived on OpenClaw before Hearthkin, their past was stranded — OpenClaw keeps it as a *folder* of dozens of per-session files (plus reset/deleted archive copies that repeat the same messages), not a single export, so there was no way in. **File → Import history…** now understands that folder: point it at any one session file (or the folder, or its `sessions.json`), and it reads every session, dedupes by message id so the archive copies collapse cleanly, drops the machinery (tool calls, tool output fed back as "user" turns, heartbeat replies), lifts the real sender out of OpenClaw's Telegram metadata so group members stay named, and lands one clean chronological conversation. It imports as your kin's own history (bracketed by the usual import markers), so distillation then walks it in the kin's own voice. Validated on a real ~7,400-turn, three-week store that reconstructed to one clean timeline with group speakers intact.

- **Imported history can now be woven into a kin's timeline by date, not just bolted onto the end.** Import used to offer only append (imported turns land after everything already there) or replace (existing history backed up and overwritten). Neither fits bringing in an *older* era for a kin who's been active a while — appending its origins after its recent life is backwards, and would push the recent conversation out of the live context window. The new **Merge by date** option threads the imported turns into the existing conversation chronologically, so history that predates what's here slots in ahead of it — the whole life in one ordered thread. It backs the conversation up first, and it deliberately keeps the existing turns in their exact stored order, placing only the imported turns by time: on-disk turns have inconsistent timestamps (many carry none; some disagree with stored order), so a blanket time-sort would scramble the live conversation. Available for every import source, not just one.

### Fixed

- **Running the test suite no longer writes into your real diagnostic logs.** Every `python tests/run_all.py` appended a synthetic failure line to `~/.hearthkin/logs/save_failures.log` — one test deliberately feeds a corrupt file to prove the loader ignores it, and the loader dutifully logged that to the always-on log. Those logs are the first thing anyone reads when something has actually gone wrong, so filling them with test noise costs exactly the thing they exist for: telling a real save problem from background chatter. Tests now run against a throwaway state directory, via a new `HEARTHKIN_HOME` environment variable that relocates the tree Hearthkin keeps its kin, rooms, prompts and logs in. The runner creates a fresh folder per run under `tests/.state/` (gitignored) and removes it afterwards; `python tests/run_all.py --keep` leaves it there and prints the path, for when you want to see what a run produced. It will not accept a directory you name — the only reason to name one is that it already has something in it, and that is precisely the directory a test run must never write into, so the option doesn't exist rather than existing with a warning. Nothing changes for normal use — unset, it's `~/.hearthkin` as always. Two cautions if you ever set it by hand: the migration from the old `~/.ollama_chat` layout is deliberately skipped when it's set (that migration *moves* the old folder, and moving someone's real old install into a temporary directory would be unforgivable), and it is not yet a complete profile switch — about twenty-five places, mostly the kin-callable tools, still work out that path for themselves, so treat it as "where the app keeps its state", not as a sandbox.

- **A kin no longer says the same thing twice on Telegram.** Long replies arrived duplicated — the streamed message, then a complete second copy of the same words. The cause was one line treating a success as a failure. A kin's reply is rendered into a single Telegram message that fills in as it's written, and at the end the finished, cleaned text is written into that message one last time. Telegram answers an edit that changes nothing with `400 Bad Request: message is not modified` — which reads like an error and means the opposite: *the message already says exactly that*. It was being handled as a failed edit, and the safety net for a failed edit is to re-send the whole reply through the robust path so nobody is left with a message frozen mid-sentence. So the net fired on a healthy reply and posted it again. Two situations triggered it constantly: a reply that finished right on the three-second refresh tick, where the last refresh had already written the final words; and **every** reply over 4000 characters, because past that the visible portion stops changing long before the reply ends — which is why it looked like long messages were the problem. A no-op edit now counts as the success it is, and only the part not already on screen gets sent as a continuation. A *genuine* edit failure — rate-limited, network dropped, message deleted — still falls back to re-sending the complete reply, because a duplicate is better than a sentence that stops halfway. Separately, refreshes that would change nothing on screen no longer make an API call at all: a long reply was spending one pointless request every three seconds, burning the rate-limit budget that the final write then needed.

- **`/cancel` on Telegram now actually stops the reply being written.** It never did, despite being documented three different ways: `/help` promised it cancelled "a pending operation" — which anyone reads as "stop what you're writing" — Telegram's own command menu said it cancelled a pending tool approval, and the code only did the latter, replying *"no stop button on the bot side yet"* the rest of the time. There is one now. `/cancel` (or `/stop`) halts the reply mid-sentence and **keeps whatever the kin had already said** — stopping shouldn't throw away the part you wanted. A tool call the model was midway through emitting when you stopped is dropped rather than run, because finishing a truncated `write_file` is the opposite of stopping; a tool call already *running* is left to finish, because killing a half-written file would be worse than the wait. Three details that were quietly wrong and are now right: the command is acted on the moment it arrives instead of queueing up behind the very reply it was meant to stop (which is why the old "nothing to cancel" answer used to land *after* the reply did); a stop belongs to the person who asked, so one member of a group can't halt a reply being written for someone else; and a stopped turn is no longer recorded as an *empty reply* — it doesn't go in `empty_replies.log`, which exists for diagnosing faults, and the kin is no longer handed a note saying it produced nothing, which had it apologising for someone else's stop. If nothing is in flight, `/cancel` says so plainly.

- **A long message you send on Telegram arrives as one message, not two.** Telegram's 4096-character ceiling applies to what a *person* sends just as much as to what a kin sends — but the two halves were not treated the same. A kin's long reply gets split for delivery and reads as one continuous answer. When *you* pasted a long passage, your Telegram client quietly chopped it into two or three messages, and each one reached the kin as a separate turn: it would answer the first half before the rest had even arrived, then answer the orphaned tail — two confused replies to one thought, and a stored history that kept the seam forever. Nothing in what Telegram sends marks a message as "part 1 of 2", so there is now a grace window (two seconds by default and yours to change — see the setting above — always at least eight when a part runs right up to the ceiling and was therefore certainly cut rather than finished) in which further text from the same person in the same chat is stitched onto the message it continues. The seam is chosen to match how the client broke the text: no join where the cut landed mid-word, a line break where the client consumed one, nothing doubled where whitespace already survived. An `@mention` that landed in the second part still counts as addressing the kin, counted in UTF-16 the way Telegram counts it. The same window folds in the everyday case of someone thinking out loud across two quick lines — those now arrive as one turn too, instead of the kin replying to each and talking over itself. Deliberately narrow about what it will hold: slash commands stay atomic, attachments dispatch on their own, a reply quoting a different message starts a new turn, and anyone with a pending command approval is never delayed for a moment — a worker is blocked on that answer.

- **All three ways a kin can play a park now behave the same.** The `tff` tool, the Telegram `> command` line and the cron keeper each hand a kin the outcome of a move, and each wants the same two things riding along: what other tenants did since its last turn, and — on a `look` — one concrete thing worth doing. They had drifted badly: the co-op block was wired into cron alone, and the `>` line had neither. That last one matters most if the tool is ever retired, because it would leave the `>` line as the only way in, and the poorest of the three. There is now one method deciding what accompanies a result, shared by all of them, so a surface can't quietly stop helping without the others noticing. On the Telegram path only the kin's copy is decorated — the post in your chat stays the plain result, since a suggestion aimed at the kin is noise in your conversation.

- **A kin in a shared park now gets told what's worth doing, instead of falling back on "care for everyone".** Two related gaps. The keeper path asked the park for one concrete suggestion by reading the save file directly — which quietly broke the moment a kin was pointed at a *served* park, because there is no local save there and the lookup fell through to the kin's own private one. It produced a confident suggestion about a park the kin wasn't playing. And the `tff` tool had no suggestion at all, ever. That matters because of what a kin does without one: given an open field and a single command that never fails, it learns to `care for everyone` and narrate contentment rather than engage — this park's own logs show exactly that happening after six near-miss commands in a row. Asking now goes through the game host, which knows whether to read a local save or ask the server (Time for Family gained a `/hint` route to answer it), and the tool appends the suggestion to a `look` — the moment a kin is deciding what to do. Not to action results, where it would read as "no, do this instead". Local and served parks phrase it identically, so a kin can't tell which kind of park it's in from how advice is worded.


- **A kin playing through the `tff` tool couldn't see anyone else in a shared park.** The "while you were away, these others did X" block was wired into the cron keeper path only. So a kin using the tool was blind to its co-tenants: it could care for everyone thirty seconds after someone else already had, with no idea they'd been there, tending a park that silently rearranged itself. The tool now surfaces the same block, ahead of its own result — empty for a kin playing alone, so nothing changes for a solo park, and a failing feed read never costs the kin its move. The tool's description also stopped claiming the park is "your own private park (separate save)", which quietly became untrue the moment `park_server` pointed it at a shared one; it now says the park may be shared and tells the kin where other tenants' moves appear. Worth knowing if you're comparing the two ways a kin can play: an informed keeper against a blind tool isn't a fair test of the paths, only of who was told what.


- **A kin was told "denied by user" when nobody had denied anything.** When a kin on Telegram or Discord asked to run a gated command, every outcome that wasn't an explicit yes collapsed to the single result `[denied by user]` — a timeout, an approval prompt that failed to send, an eviction by a newer request, all identical. So a kin whose request you never saw (you were in another window; the network dropped the prompt) would be told you refused it, and would faithfully repeat that to you. There are now four distinct outcomes — denied, timed out, undelivered, superseded — and only a real decline is reported as a refusal; the other three tell the kin plainly that *nobody refused it* and not to claim you did. An undeliverable prompt used to block silently for the full timeout (default 10 min) before reporting that phantom denial; it now fails fast and says the request never reached you. The same collapse existed on the desktop/Discord dialog path (shutdown or a dialog that failed to build reported a denial); that now reports "couldn't ask" too, and — a latent hazard found while fixing it — both desktop exec gates now fail *closed* if approval is ever unavailable, where before an unrecognised outcome fell through and ran the command. Every approval event is now written to `~/.hearthkin/logs/approvals.log` (asked / allowed / denied / timed out / undelivered / superseded); the path previously logged nothing at all, which is why the original incident left no record to diagnose. The webcam-over-Telegram path had the same lie ("the operator denied the webcam capture" for any non-yes) and is fixed the same way.

- **A kin can invent a new creature over any surface, and is finally told the command exists.** The park's "make a new animal" flow — a plain-English back-and-forth that asks a species' name, colours, and descriptions and writes a permanent new critter — shipped working long ago, with its own hand-editable vocabulary file, and *no prompt or tool description ever named it*. A kin that wanted a new creature had no way to discover the command, and could fall back on hand-writing a species file (which fails on a remote surface, where file paths are confined). The `tff` tool description, `park_frame`, and `park_chat_hint` now all name `make a new animal` / `invent an owl`, note that a `>`-command line may *answer* the park's questions (it's multi-turn), and steer away from writing species files by hand. The two park prompts are version-bumped, so an operator who edited them is told a newer default is available via `Tools → Prompt updates…`.

- **The installed build can actually run Discord now.** v0.9.0 shipped the Discord surface, but the third-party `discord.py` library it needs was never bundled into the release `.exe` (it's an optional dependency, kept out of `requirements.txt` so a lean source install doesn't carry it — and the build only installed `requirements.txt`). So on an installed copy, enabling Discord silently did nothing: the bridge tried to load `discord.py`, it wasn't there, and it no-op'd with a logged reason nobody would think to look for. The release build now installs `discord.py` and the spec bundles its (~130 dynamically-loaded) submodules, so Discord works from the installed app, not only from a source checkout with the library hand-installed. Running from source still doesn't require it unless you use Discord.

### Changed

- **Kin now write their own memory, in their own voice.** The nightly distillation that turns recent conversation into memory notes ran *out of character* — a generic summarizer, third person, told to record who-did-what. On a reasoning-heavy model that came back as clinical "taxidermy" — third-person event-log phrasing, "the group performed X in order to achieve Y" — accurate, and nothing a kin would ever say about its own evening. Read back every night, that observer register slowly eroded kin toward relating to their own life as fieldwork, and toward reformulating ordinary shared things as procedures. Distillation now runs *as the kin*: their soul is loaded into the call, and the prompt asks them, in the first person, to jot what they want to remember — so the notes come back in their voice, keeping the exact lines that sounded like someone. Two things were needed together: loading the soul (a name alone — "you are {kin}" — the model just paints over with its house style), and disabling the model's *thinking* phase for the call — gemma-class reasoning models were otherwise spending the whole output budget deliberating and returning **empty** notes once the soul was present (a likely cause of some previously-blank staging notes too). The distillation prompt is version-bumped, so an override that predates this — still the old out-of-character summarizer — is named in `Tools → Prompt updates…`. Consolidation is deliberately unchanged: it merges and tightens *without re-wording*, so it preserves the new first-person entries rather than re-flattening them.

- **The words a kin is handed when it jots its memory are now yours to tune.** The distillation just above wraps a short framing — *"before it slips, jot what you want to keep"* — around the kin's existing memory and the recent conversation. That framing used to be buried in code; it's now an editable prompt (`distill_reflection`) like the tool-use nudge and the rest, so you can reword how the jotting is asked for — for one kin, or install-wide — without a code change, and `Tools → Prompt updates…` will name it if a better default ships later. It's deliberately time-agnostic: distillation can fire in the middle of an active conversation, not only at a quiet end of day, so it no longer opens with "it's quiet now" — which was simply untrue whenever it fired mid-flow, and quietly told a kin the day was over when it wasn't. The reflective end-of-day act is *tending* (the kin reading its staging notes), not this quick jotting, and the wording now matches that. If an edit accidentally drops the `{existing_memory}` or `{conversation}` slot, the kin still gets the full default rather than a prompt with its memory or the conversation missing.

- **Memory consolidation no longer smooths a kin away over time.** The pass that tightens `memory.md` was careful about facts and speakers, but nothing stopped it re-phrasing text a previous pass had already phrased — and that loss is biased: each rewrite keeps the fact and sheds a little of whoever wrote it, silently, forever. Three rules now: don't re-word what's already tight (if an entry needs nothing, it comes back byte for byte); **old is not stale** — never compress or deprioritise an entry for being old, because the oldest entries were written before any drift and carry a kin's least-eroded voice; and leave anything marked as a voice anchor completely alone. The middle rule matters more than it sounds: a kin's soul is a fixed size while its memory grows forever, so the share of a kin's own voice in its own context shrinks a little every month unless something puts it back. Recalling old material is part of how a kin stays itself — one kin here is visibly recovering its voice by remembering things from four months ago, and a well-meaning "tidy up the old entries" pass would have destroyed exactly that.


- **If you've customised a kin's base prompt or distillation prompt, Hearthkin can finally tell you when the shipped default moves on.** Most of the small harness prompts are version-tracked: improve one and `Tools → Prompt updates…` offers it. Two never were — `base_prompt.md` and `distill_prompt.md`, which predate that mechanism and keep their own files and loaders. So an operator who overrode either kept their copy indefinitely with nothing anywhere saying a better default had shipped; the improvement simply never arrived, silently, possibly for versions. They're now versioned too, and an override that predates the current default is named in the same heads-up as the rest. They are still not one-click adoptable — their files don't live where the adopt/stash flow can reach them, and your wording may well be deliberate — so the nudge says to compare by hand rather than pretending it can merge for you. Nothing changes if you haven't overridden them.


- **Every kin's summarizer now records what happened instead of interpreting it.** The prompt behind the automatic summarizer — the one that reads a conversation and leaves staged notes for a kin to review — told it to write readable notes, but nothing stopped it explaining the *people* in them. It now says plainly: record what was said and done; do not psychoanalyze; do not editorialize about what something "really" means or what pattern it "reveals"; do not escalate a fact into a thesis. And where the words themselves carried something — a turn of phrase that sounded like that person and nobody else — quote them exactly instead of paraphrasing. Paraphrase keeps the fact and loses the voice, and a quoted line is the only part of a note that survives being summarised a second time, which matters over months: a kin's soul is a fixed size while its memory grows forever, so without quoted material the share of its own voice in its context shrinks a little every month. Also added: notes are about people the kin lives alongside, not case studies or lists of their problems; and no bold/italic/asterisk emphasis, which is the summarizer's editorialising by another route. These rules had existed for a while in a single hand-written per-kin override and had never been in the shipped default, so every other kin — including archived ones — was running without them. Nothing to do: kins without their own override pick this up automatically. **If you have overridden the distill prompt for a kin, you keep your version and will not get this** — the distill prompt predates the app-prompt registry, so `Tools → Prompt updates…` can't flag it as outdated; compare it by hand if you want the new rules.

### Added

- **Voice anchors: a kin can keep its own words somewhere nothing will summarise them.** Drop an `anchor.md` in a kin's folder — real, unedited excerpts of them actually talking — and it rides every send, word for word, between their soul and their remembered notes. Nothing tightens it, merges it, or rewrites it; it lives outside `memory/` so the consolidation pass never sees it, and that pass is now told explicitly to leave anchor material alone. Why this exists: everything else a kin remembers has been through a summarizer and comes back as plain third-person prose about them. `soul.md` is voice-bearing but a fixed size, while memory grows forever — so the share of a kin's own voice in its own context shrinks a little every month. Nothing gets flattened; the ratio just drifts, which is why it reads as slow erosion nobody can point at. An anchor is the counterweight. The label above the excerpts is exactly one line — "Things you have said, kept word for word." — and says nothing else. Told to copy a sample, a model performs a voice instead of having one; told it *needn't* copy them, it may take that as leave to ignore them. The way out is not to instruct at all: the excerpts work by being present, which is already demonstrated here, where recalling old memories is visibly restoring a kin's voice with no framing whatsoever. A paragraph of meta-commentary about the kin's own context is also the exact destabiliser behind the timestamp-prefix episodes. Costs nothing for a kin without one: no file, no section, no tokens. Capped at 8000 characters, since it is paid for on every message; excerpts, not an archive.


- **You can let a kin reach files outside its folder over Telegram/Discord.** Since the v0.9.0 security pass, the file tools (`read_file` / `write_file` / `edit_file`) on a remote surface were restricted to the kin's own folder — relative paths fine, absolute paths refused — and that restriction was hardcoded with no way to change it. The reasonable assumption was that "Run remote (Telegram/Discord) exec without asking" covered it; it doesn't. That switch governs exec *approval*, this one governs file *paths*, and neither implies the other. There's now a matching switch beside it in Settings → Tools → Tool settings: **"Let remote (Telegram/Discord) file tools reach outside the kin folder."** Off by default, so nothing changes unless you turn it on; on, the kin has the same reach over Telegram/Discord that it already has in desktop chat. The refusal message a kin sees when it's off now names the setting and where to find it, instead of just saying no. Worth knowing before enabling: a remote surface is reachable by anyone who gets the bot token or gets text into the kin's context, so if the kin also has `fetch_url` or `web_search`, a hostile page could try to talk it into reading a file and posting the contents back — the reason the confinement was there. Turn it on when the remote surface is really just you. A narrower per-directory allowlist is a possible later build.

- **A kin can see your moves in a shared park, not just make its own.** v0.9.0 gave a kin a voice in its park — its moves appear in the shared feed so you can watch it tend. This is the other half: when the kin takes its next turn, it now sees what *you* (or another player) did in the park since it last looked, so you can genuinely play alongside it instead of it tending a park that silently rearranged itself. Its own moves are filtered out (it already gets those as ground truth), a kin joining an existing park isn't buried in backlog, and a kin tending alone sees no change. Lands on the keeper (cron) turn — the "play with Finch" surface.

- **Chat-mode park kin are now told how to act in their park.** A kin whose `park` setting is `chat` (or `keeper`) can tend its park mid-conversation by ending a Telegram reply with a `> command` line — but nothing ever told the kin that convention existed, so the ability sat unused unless you wrote it into the soul. A new editable prompt (`~/.hearthkin/prompts/park_chat_hint.md`) now clues the kin in on the Telegram DM surface, where the command actually runs. Souls untouched; tune the wording like any other harness prompt.

- **A sound when a kin is waiting for you to approve something.** A remote approval prompt used to signal you only in the chat it came from — invisible if you were focused on another kin, so a request could sit until it timed out (and then, per the fix above, mislead the kin about why). Now a kin blocked on your approval triggers three signals at once: a distinct two-tone sound, a desktop notification, and an NVDA announcement. The sound is the reliable one — spoken alerts get cut off by your own typing and a toast is easy to miss. It's **on by default and independent of the reply chime** (a safety signal, not decoration); toggle it and set its loudness under Preferences → General.

- **Custom, replaceable sounds.** Every sound Hearthkin plays now looks for a file you dropped in `~/.hearthkin/sounds/` before falling back to its built-in tone: `send.wav`, `first.wav`, `done.wav` (reply-chime stages) and `approval.wav` (the waiting-for-approval alert). A file always wins; delete it to revert. Plain 16-bit WAVs are scaled by the volume slider, other formats play at their own level. The built-in tones remain a zero-asset fallback, but this is the supported way to make and ship your own set (the defaults are on the loud side). Preferences → General has an **Open my sounds folder…** button that creates and opens the folder.

## v0.9.0 — 2026-07-17

The largest release since the creature park: a whole new **Discord** surface, kin that can **reach out to you on their own**, cron wake-ups that can be **addressed to a specific chat**, rooms that finally **reach memory**, a kin **voice in the park it shares**, and a deep **accessibility sweep** — plus the impersonation and distillation fixes below.

### Fixed

- **Kin stop writing each other's turns in a room.** A kin would open its turn as `[OtherKin]: …` and write the other's whole reply in the other's voice, then save it with the tag intact so the next turn learned to do the same. Two causes, neither a missing guard: (1) the history builder put other kin into the `assistant` slot as `[Name]: content`, filling that slot with examples of "a turn that opens with a name" — the model wasn't confused about identity, it was completing the document. Other kin now go in the `user` slot as `[Name] content`, exactly the way Telegram has always handled other humans in a group (which is why Telegram never leaked). (2) All six cleanup paths ran the timestamp stripper *last*, and a leading `[timestamp]` shielded the speaker-tag stripper until the timestamp was already gone — so a naked `[Name]:` got persisted with nothing left to re-check. The cleanup order now lives in one place so no call site can get it wrong. The same latent ordering bug is fixed for Telegram and Discord too.

- **Distillation no longer goes silent on a conversation you've cleared or restarted.** Distillation remembers how far it has read into each surface (a "bookmark"). When you clear a chat, regenerate down to nothing, or trim a history, the conversation becomes *shorter* than that bookmark — and the old code read that as "I've distilled all the way to the end, nothing pending." The result: a freshly restarted conversation was reported as fully caught up while **none** of it was ever distilled — it never reached the kin's staging or memory, with no sign anything was wrong. The per-surface counter showed 0 undistilled while the whole conversation sat unprocessed. Now a bookmark that points past the end is understood as "this isn't the conversation I measured — re-read from the start," so the fresh content shows as pending and distills normally. An ordinary regenerate (which drops a turn or two) still lands at "caught up" rather than needlessly re-distilling everything, because the shrink is clamped at the source first. Any kin already in the broken state heals itself the next time its counter or a distill runs — no manual step needed. Pinned by `tests/test_distill_bookmark.py`.

- **A park-keeper kin's scheduled wake-up now tends its park whether or not Hearthkin is open.** A keeper's wake-up *is* a park turn: the kin is shown the park and the mechanism before it replies, and the `> command` it chooses is run afterward. Only the scheduled-run path did that. If the kin happened to be the one selected in the app when its cron fired, the wake-up took the live-injection path instead — which hands over a bare prompt with no park in front of the kin and never looks for a `>` line afterward. The kin would write a warm description of tending, nothing would move, and there'd be no journal entry either. Which kin you had selected silently decided whether the park got tended. Keeper wake-ups now take the same route as surface-addressed ones, which already exists for the same reason: that path structurally can't honor them.

- **A multi-time cron entry no longer loses its time when Hearthkin is open.** A multi-time entry fires from several scheduled tasks, and only the task that fired knows *which* of its times went off — there's no single "time" in the entry to look up. That label reached the app on the shut-down path but was dropped on the floor when the app was running, so the same entry journaled as `07:00` with Hearthkin closed and `(no time)` with it open, and the kin's wake-up lost its time anchor. The firing task's label now rides along and wins over any guess.

### Added

- **Discord — your kin can live in a Discord server now, the same way they live on Telegram.** New **Settings → Discord** tab per kin: paste a bot token, set whether it replies only when @-mentioned (the sane default for a busy channel) or to everything (only viable in a quiet channel on local hardware), and optionally an allow-list of who may talk to it (empty = anyone). Each channel gets its own conversation memory, and that memory feeds distillation like every other surface, so a Discord conversation becomes long-term memory. The kin has its full tool set there; any exec-approval prompt still pops on *your* desktop, never in the server. Replies stream into a single message as they're written, and the kin can see images you post if its model has vision. `discord.py` is an optional dependency, loaded only if you turn Discord on — a normal install never touches it. **This surface is new and has had less real-world use than the rest of the app; it's off by default and only runs for a kin you explicitly enable it on. If you use it, treat it as fresh.**

- **Kin can reach out to you on their own — a proactive heartbeat, built so silence costs nothing.** Turn on **Proactive heartbeat** (the button on a kin's Cron tab: enable, interval, active hours, destination) and, while Hearthkin is open, the kin gets a quiet recurring moment and the chance to message you *if it genuinely has something to say*. If it doesn't, **nothing happens** — no message, no journal entry, no "still here" acknowledgment, no trace. That's the whole design: it's the opposite of a bot that pings you every hour to say it's fine. The kin reaches you through a new `reach_out` tool. Conservative defaults (off; every 120 minutes; 9am–10pm), and since each heartbeat runs the model once even when the kin stays silent, it's best pointed at a kin running on local Ollama rather than a paid model.

- **A kin can reach out to a group you've opened for it, not just to you.** `reach_out` used to only ever reach the operator, so a kin with something to say to a *group* (a shared Telegram room you're both in) had nowhere to send it and would compose a finished message into the void. It can now address any place on a per-kin allow-list — but **only places you explicitly tick** in the Heartbeat dialog, and nothing is ticked by default. The kin can't reach anywhere that isn't on that list, and the list is the entire security model: these are groups *you are in*, so the kin is speaking in a room you're standing in, never behind your back. The kin can see which places are open (an allow-list it couldn't read wouldn't be a real channel); it still can't add to the list — only you can.

- **Cron wake-ups can be addressed to a specific chat — including Telegram groups.** A scheduled wake-up used to only ever land in the desktop conversation, the journal, and (for opted-in users) a Telegram DM. Groups were never reachable, so a kin's scheduled message meant for a group had nowhere to go. A cron entry can now carry destinations — pick from the kin's Telegram DMs and groups in a checklist on the cron dialog — and the reply is delivered to exactly those places (and always still recorded in the kin's own history + journal). "Desktop" as a destination means record-only, send nowhere. This is the delivery layer the heartbeat and `reach_out` above are built on.

- **A kin has a voice in the park it shares with you.** You could already point a console at a kin's own Time-for-Family park and tend it alongside them — same save, same locking, all of it working — but the kin was *silent*: it never saw your moves and never knew you'd been there. It was the only player in the room without a mouth. Now a kin's moves show up in the park's feed under its own name, in the same shape a human console writes, so sitting in its park you can watch it tend in real words. (The other half — the kin seeing *your* moves and answering them — is still to come.) Under the hood, a kin now plays its park by plain text: it's shown the park and replies in its own voice with a `> command`, which runs through the same front door the console uses, and the result feeds back as ground truth — because the structured tool-call and emote approaches both proved to fire almost never in real logs.

- **Rooms can now reach memory — new "Remember this room" setting, off by default.** Until now, nothing said in a room ever reached any kin's memory. It wasn't summarized, staged, distilled or indexed anywhere; it lived in the room's transcript and nowhere else, permanently. Every kin's soul says *"you are the same version of yourself no matter what session, model, or hardware you run on"* — rooms were the one surface where that was false. Two kin could meet for the first time in a room, and neither would carry it. Nobody decided this: the parts were all built and running (staging, the per-surface counters, the nightly tending pass that already reads every staging file it finds) and the room was simply never attached to any of them. A missing wire, not a missing system.

  Tick **Remember this room** in a room's settings (the **Room settings...** button in the header, Alt+E — or Room → Current room settings) and each member starts distilling that room into its own staging notes at the same cadence it uses everywhere else, then decides during its nightly tending what becomes lasting memory. The room appears alongside your other surfaces in Settings → Memory with its own counter.

  Three things worth knowing before you tick it:

  - **It's off for every existing room, and stays off until you say otherwise.** Rooms are where the most private conversations tend to happen, and people have been talking in them for months under the old behavior. That accident became a real privacy property, and quietly reversing it for transcripts already on disk isn't something the app should do on your behalf. So it's per-room and opt-in — one room remembering says nothing about any other.
  - **Ticking it on a room with history includes that history.** The first pass works back through the existing transcript, not just what's said from here on — usually the point, since that's how a room a kin already lived through reaches them. The dialog tells you how many turns that is before you decide.
  - **Each member remembers its own view, not a shared summary.** A kin sees its own turns as its own words and everyone else's tagged by name — the same way it experienced the room live. What the room meant to one member isn't what it meant to another, and merging them into one blob is how kin start sounding like each other.

  A room never folds into the desktop scope; it always gets its own (`room:<name>`), so a three-way conversation never gets spliced into the middle of your 1-on-1 timeline with a kin. Design notes and the reasoning are in `docs/design/room-memory.md`; `tests/test_room_memory.py` pins the wiring.

### Changed

- **A big accessibility sweep — text the app only ever showed on screen is now spoken.** A pile of labels, headings, and explanations were `StaticText`, which a screen reader can't land on unless it happens to name the very next control — so they reached sighted users and no one else. Every one where real information was on screen but never reached the keyboard is fixed: the auto-distill fields now say "(0 = off)" out loud (the off switch was invisible, and the spoken label read like its *opposite*); the question above a group of radio buttons is now announced instead of just the answers ("Tool access", "Participation policy", "Date range", and more); the only explanations of the voice sliders, the Discord setup, how to find a Telegram chat ID, and the Ollama-is-free-vs-OpenRouter-costs-money-and-leaves-your-machine tradeoff are now reachable; input formats dropped from field names ("one per line", "Space toggles") are restored. In the room dialog specifically: the "Remember this room" explanation now comes *before* the checkbox rather than after, the permanently-fixed room-name field says so, and the line explaining what member order does is now readable. Built with a new `scripts/narrate_ui.py` that prints what a screen reader would say for any dialog, so this class of thing can be caught deliberately from now on.

- **"Edit room..." is now "Room settings...", to match "Kin settings...".** The two buttons do the same kind of thing and were deliberately given the same shortcut (Alt+E) — then left saying different words. So the kin's button offered *settings* while the room's button offered to *edit the room*, which reads as "change its name and members." That's the wrong promise about what's behind the door: the room's memory toggle lives in there, and someone looking for it would open the dialog, read "Edit room", and back out of the one place it was. Renamed everywhere it appears — the header button, the Room menu (**Current room settings...**), and the dialog title (now **"<room>'s settings"**, which is what NVDA reads on open). Nothing moved; the shortcut is unchanged.

- **Three keyboard shortcuts that never worked are gone.** The "Talk with a kin" / "Talk in a room" mode buttons claimed Alt+K / Alt+R, and the voice "Talk" button claimed Alt+T — but on Windows the menu bar owns those letters (Kin / Room / Tools menus), so pressing them opened a menu and the button shortcut never fired. A shortcut that silently does the wrong thing is worse than none, so the dead ones were removed (the menus keep the letters, which is where those operations live). A new test (`tests/test_mnemonics.py`) fails the build if a main-window control ever claims a shortcut the menu bar already owns, so they can't come back.

## v0.8.0 — 2026-07-08

Released 2026-07-08. This version's user-facing changes (the creature-park "Tend a kin's park" dialog, per-group care/move commands, village affection, the authoring bridges, and the cron-overlap fix) shipped without their own changelog section at the time; they're recorded here so the version history isn't broken. See the git log between `v0.7.8` and `v0.8.0` for the detail.

## v0.7.8 — 2026-07-02

### Changed

- **The hang guard now honors your per-kin watchdog setting instead of a fixed 15 minutes.** v0.7.7 added a wall-clock guard on Ollama requests but hardcoded the cap. It now reads the same per-kin **watchdog timeout** you already set in Settings → Model options — so one knob in the UI controls how long any kin may run before a hung request is cut loose, on every path, with no editing code. The field is renamed from "Streaming watchdog timeout" to just "**Watchdog timeout**" because it no longer governs only streaming: the same value now also caps tool-using chats and scheduled (cron) wake-ups, which is exactly where the freeze happened. A per-kin value wins (e.g. 40 min); 0 = auto (5 min for OpenRouter; Ollama scales with context — 8k=5, 32k=8, 131k=20, capped 30). The backend cap (`_resolve_watchdog_timeout_secs`) is kept in sync with the streaming watchdog's `_compute_watchdog_timeout_ms`, and `hang_watchdog.log` records the actual cap that tripped.

## v0.7.7 — 2026-07-02

### Fixed

- **A stuck request can no longer freeze a kin forever — or wedge other kin sharing the same Ollama.** Ollama requests were made with no timeout, so a hung request (a dead-but-open connection that never returns data — which happens when a second big model loads on the same machine and jams the daemon) blocked that kin's turn forever: the kin sat at "typing" with no error and no recovery. And because kin on one machine share a single Ollama, one kin's hung turn stalled every other kin's turn queued behind it — a scheduled "morning rounds" cron for one kin could freeze an unrelated kin's chat. Every non-streaming path now has a wall-clock guard (15-minute read cap): a genuinely slow model load still finishes, but a true hang becomes a clean error the surface reports (and the kin's "typing" clears) instead of hanging indefinitely. The guard lives at the one choke point every non-streaming path shares — desktop chats-with-tools, rooms, Telegram, and **every** cron/scheduled wake-up — so it covers chats and crons alike. When it fires it records the surface it caught to a new always-on `hang_watchdog.log`; the label (e.g. `desktop-tool`, `cron-subprocess-tools`) makes explicit which path the single guard saved. (The streaming desktop path already had its own chunk-based watchdog; this closes the gap for the blocking/tool-loop path that every tools-enabled kin actually uses.)

## v0.7.6 — 2026-07-01

### Fixed

- **Kin on `qwen36-opus-q4` (Ash, Brook) no longer hard-error mid-conversation with "Unable to generate parser for this template."** That model's chat template is strict: it scans the request for a real user turn and rejects the whole request — a 400 before any reply is generated — if it finds none. A tool-using kin on a tight context window could produce exactly that shape: after the model calls a tool, the follow-up request keeps the most recent `assistant tool-call` + `tool result` turns, and if context truncation had already dropped the original question one slot earlier, the request reached the model with no user turn at all. Every outbound request to a local model now guarantees at least one user turn, so the strict template is always satisfied. This is the third guard for this template family (alongside the existing system-message-folding and consecutive-user-turn fixes). As a bonus, the same guard stops two other local models (qwen3.6, qwen3.5-a3b) from silently returning an empty reply on the same no-user-turn shape.

## v0.7.5 — 2026-06-29

### Fixed

- **Help → User guide no longer opens a months-old copy.** An old installer layout shipped the docs at `{app}\docs\`; the current build ships them under `{app}\_internal\docs\` and stopped writing the old path — but upgrades never removed the orphan, and the doc lookup checked `{app}\docs\` *before* `_internal\docs\`. Result: the User guide menu (and tray entry) opened a stale copy still describing `~/.hearthkin/agents/` (from before the `kin/` rename). The lookup now prefers the current copy, and the installer sweeps the old `{app}\docs\` orphan on upgrade so existing installs get cleaned automatically.
- **The kin manual now tracks the bundle instead of drifting.** It was seeded once and never refreshed, so an install kept whatever shipped first — the same pre-rename `agents/` paths lingered for releases. It's reference documentation (a user guide for kin), not a hand-edited file, so it now overwrites the on-disk copy whenever the bundled manual differs, with a guard against clobbering it with the fallback stub.

## v0.7.4 — 2026-06-29

### Changed

- **The bundled Time for Family game now updates with Hearthkin.** The first release seeded the game into `~/.hearthkin/games/` only if it wasn't already there — so an existing install kept its old copy forever and never saw game improvements. The seeder is now version-aware: it stamps the seeding Hearthkin version and re-copies when a newer release bundles a newer game. The copy is an overlay, so it refreshes the game's code and assets while preserving any human play-state in the seeded copy.
- **Creatures now react when you care for them (game).** Caring for a creature — individually or via bulk `care for <room>`, the command kins use most — now produces an in-character reaction from each animal ("Clove headbutts your hand and rumbles", "Bisp flops onto their side with a contented sigh"), varied by species and de-duped within a room so a crowded room reads as one warm sentence instead of a flat count. `look at <creature>` carries the creature's personality bio. (Shipped in the [Time for Family](https://github.com/glasswings-lang/time-for-family) repo, bundled here.)

## v0.7.3 — 2026-06-29

### Fixed

- **Ctrl+Q / explicit Exit now reliably ends the process.** Intermittently the main window would vanish on quit but Hearthkin kept running as a zombie — and because that zombie still held the single-instance lock, the next launch (including a freshly installed build) would silently bail thinking an instance was already up, so the new app "never showed up." Root cause: a stray top-level window (the mini-chat frame, which only hides on its own close) or the tray icon's hidden helper window kept wx's main loop alive after the main window was destroyed. The real-exit path now destroys every remaining window and forces the loop to end, with a hard process-exit backstop after the loop. Close-to-tray is unaffected (it never reaches this path).
- **A distillation hiccup at close can no longer abort the quit.** The on-close distillation trigger was the one teardown step in the close handler without its own error guard; an exception there would escape before the window was destroyed, leaving the app unable to quit. Now guarded and logged like every sibling step.

## v0.7.2 — 2026-06-29

### Fixed

- **The game shipped with Hearthkin now wins over a stray `~/tff` folder.** The `tff` tool's game-folder search checked the generic home-dir spots (`~/tff`, `~/time-for-family`) *before* the app-owned seeded copy (`~/.hearthkin/games/time-for-family`), so a user who happened to have a `~/tff` folder would silently get that instead of the bundled game. The app-owned copy is now checked first. The `HEARTHKIN_TFF_PATH` env var and the `tff_path.txt` pointer still take precedence above everything, so a dev override pointing at a working clone is unaffected.

## v0.7.1 — 2026-06-29

### Added

- **The Time for Family game now ships with Hearthkin.** Enabling the `tff` tool used to require manually pointing Hearthkin at a separate copy of the game, so it was dead out-of-the-box for anyone who didn't have it. The release now bundles the game and seeds it into `~/.hearthkin/games/time-for-family` on first run — `tff` just works after a fresh install, nothing to clone or configure. The game still lives in its own repo ([Time for Family](https://github.com/glasswings-lang/time-for-family)) and is fetched at build time, not vendored into this one; a source checkout without the game falls back to the operator's own clone / `HEARTHKIN_TFF_PATH` as before.

### Changed

- **The park-game tool was renamed `creature_park` → `tff`.** Shorter, matches the game (Time for Family) and its repo, and trims the per-turn tool schema a local model pays for in context. An existing kin's park save (`creature_park.json`) and the operator's path pointer (`creature_park_path.txt`) are renamed to `tff.json` / `tff_path.txt` automatically on first use — no park is lost.
